THE REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 


Samuet Kivpin was born at Bedford, January 4th. 
1774. His father, Mr. John Kilpin, was, during fifty 
years, an honored and active member of the church 
assembling in the old meeting-house, formerly John 
Bunyan’s, at Bedford. Thirty of these years he was 
an able, faithful deacon. He ruled his children and his 
household well. His wife was his helpmeet in that im- 
portant branch of duty. 

Their beloved child Samuel would often relate, with 
delight and gratitude, many of the scenes of his infancy 
and childhood, connected with the wise, judicious, and 
kind government maintained under his parents’ roof. 
They were anxious to form the future character of 
their beloved children, ten in number, seven of whom 
lived to be the heads of families; and they recollected 
their father’s conduct with gratitude, taking it as the 
model for their own households. Their pious mother 
made the laws of God the rule of her actions, and ex- 
erted that authority which God had given her, and for 
which she expected to render an account. “ Train up 
a child in the way he should go,” was a weighty com- 
mand on her parental heart. When a month old, the 
children were carried to the house of God, and nothing 
but illness was ever allowed as an excuse for their not 
being present during their infancy or childhood. “ Be- 


4 LIFE OF THE 


hold we are all here as a family, waiting before God,” 
was their Sabbath law. Such was the parents’ firmness 
on this point, that a dispute was never raised. The 
children, under such guides, were a family of order, 
love, and industry; the parents were the friends and 
confidants of their children; a look of disapprobation 
was a severe punishment. 

The subject of this sketch has been heard to say, that 
ne had no conception of any thing so affecting or abid- 
ing as his dear father’s method of reproof for sin. All 
the children were assembled, and seated in due order, 
the mother in the midst of her young group. The fa- 
ther would then enter with a rod in one hand, and the 
Bible in the other. He read different portions of Scrip- 
ture on the crime which had been committed, and en- 
deavored to convince them that it was a bitter thing to 
sin against God, and although the rod would not alone 
change the heart or conduct, yet God had given it in 
charge to the parent, with the assurance that he who 
spared the rod spoiled the child. All then knelt reve- 
rently, whilst the godly parent wept over his sinful 
child, and sought pardon through the blood of the Sa 
vior. This punishment was so effectual, that it was 
very seldom necessary. No children could more re- 
vere the memory of their parents, or anticipate with 
greater pleasure a joyful meeting in the regions ot 
blessedness. These tender but scriptural restraints had 
a most beneficial influence on the children’s conduct 
‘ in the future periods of their lives. They were taught 
to reverence God’s ministers, and to consider it a great 
honor to assist them in any way. To be seated on the 
knee of a man of God, or to receive a blessing from 
him, was a high honor, and an opportunity of riding 
or walking with any of them for a few miles was 


REY. SAMUEL KILPIN. § 


eagerly sought. The infirmities of good men were not 
allowed as subjects for conversation, nor was religion 
in any way lightly named in the family. 

Two or three circumstances, which made a deep 
impression on the infant mind of Samuel, may be re- 
lated. His father was an ironmonger, and kept a ge- 
neral retail shop in that line. A nobleman in the 
neighborhood was among his best customers. One 
Sabbath morning the steward came to the house, and 
said with an insolent sneer, “ Are you afraid of the 
devil, Mr. Kilpin?’ “No,” replied the good man, “I 
am not.” “Will you then sell me some articles to- 
day?” “No, I will not; it is the Sabbath day, and the 
God of the Sabbath I iove and fear. To-morrow I 
shall feel much obliged by executing his lordship’s 
orders.” “ Very well, if you will not serve me to-day, 
you shall not to-morrow, or on any other day.” The 
steward then retired in a violent rage. This scene was 
never forgotten by the young family group: and it is 
pleasing to be able to add, that the nobleman increased 
his favors when told of the circumstance. 

Passing the street one evening, a drunken man 
knocked Mr. Kilpin down, and rolled him into the 
gutter, exclaiming, “ That’s the place for you, John 
Bunyan!” The good man arose calmly, and returning 
to his family, related the circumstance; adding, “that 
the honor of bearing such a name had outweighed the 
insult.” 

Having, from some cause, displeased a member of 
ihe church at a prayer-meeting, his offended brother 
used most unbecoming expressions respecting him in 
prayer. Witt a mind quite unruffled, he said, on his 
family offeriag their sympathy, and expressing resent- 


ment, “I was not the least hurt on my own account: 
]* 


6 LIFE OF THE 


such talking never goes higher than the ceiling. The 
God of love never admits it as prayer.” ; 

Other anecdotes might be mentioned which had an 
important bearing on the future ministerial labors of 
his son, who devoted much precious time to address- 
ing parents and children on the things concerning the 
kingdom of God. Upon those occasions, frequent re- 
ferences were made to his early days beneath the 
parental roof. Being dead, his parents still spake in 
the life and exhortations of their beloved son. 

As Mr. Kilpin’s life was consecrated most actively 
to the service of his God and Savior, it will be pleasant 
to trace the period at which he became devoted to his 
Lord, and to view the early indications of grace com- 
municated from on high. Here we have his own me- 
morandums. Some were written in the margin of a 
large Bible, and escaped the flames, when he burned 
most of his papers during his last illness. He says, 
“JT remember having very strong religious feelings at 
four years of age.” 

“ Matt. 5: 22. ‘Whosoever shall say, Thou fool, shall 
be in danger of hell fire.’ Whilst at play, I once called 
my brother, ‘Fool.’ The first most powerful convic- 
tions I ever had, were from this text. I was about six 
years old, and was reading Dr. Watts’ hymn 


‘ And he’s in danger of hell fire, 
‘ That calls his brother fool.’ 


* Then,’ I exclaimed, ‘I am lost!’ and with much anger 
against the author, I threw his book into the fire. It is 
nearly forty years since that period, but I have never 
again felt angry with Dr. Watts.” 

“When seven years old, I was left in charge of the 
shop. A man passed, crying, ‘ Little lambs, all white 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 7 


and clean, at one penny each.’ In my eagerness to 
get one, I lost all self-command, and taking a penny 
from the drawer, I made the purchase. My keen- 
eyed, wise mother, inquired how I came by the money. 
Tevaded the question with something like a lie. In 
God’s sight it was a lie, as I kept back the truth. 

“The lamb was placed on the chimney-shelf, and 
much admired. To me it was a source of inexpressible 
anguish. Continually there sounded in my ears, and 
heart, ‘ Thou shalt not steal; thou shalt not lie” Guilt 
and darkness overcame my mind, and in sore agony of 
soul I went to a hay-loft, (the place is now perfectly 
in my recollection,) and there prayed and pleaded, 
with groanings that could not be uttered, for mercy 
and pardon. I entreated mercy, for Jesus’ sake. With 
joy and transport I left the Joft, from a believing appli- 
cation of the text, ‘Thy sins that are many, are for- 
given thee. I went to my mother, told her what I 
had done, and sought her forgiveness, and burnt the 
lamb, whilst she wept over her youn, penitent.” 

This he deemed the period of his conversion to God. 
The bias of his will and affections were turned to Christ. 

In another place he says, “ When I was some months 

. more than thirteen years, (I well remember the time, 
on the evening of December 4th, 1787, forty years 
ago,) I was invited to attend the Methodist love-feast. 
Mr. Jenkins preached. Deep convictions pierced my 
soul. Here I might write a volume, but, no! from that 
period I have been a poor saved sinner, a child of Pro- 
vidence.” 

His sister, younger than himself, nearly at this pe- 
riod, became a new creature in Christ Jesus. From 
her information the account of what passed that night 
is derived, She says, “At half-past nine o’clock, I 


8 LIFE OF THE 


heard a knock at the door; it was my brother Samuel. 
He entered in deep distress, exclaiming, ‘Can you, my 
father, my mother, forgive vour rebel son? He went 
to bed, but had no rest. I carcied him some water. 
' He thanked me, and observed, ‘I am afraid there will 
be a time when J shall call for a drop in vain.” That 
‘night it might be said to our parents, Salvation is come 
to your house. We were very dear to each other, and 
much united in feeling. Next to the salvation of my 
own soul, was the desire for that of my dear Samuel. 
My heart’s desire to God was for his salvation. Never, 
no, never can I forget that night, whilst memory lasts. 
The effects will endure throughout eternity. For twelve 
months he was under the power of dreadful tempta- 
tions; his mind was so harassed, that peace had left his 
bosom, not to be restored, until Christ revealed himself 
to his wounded soul, as the hope of glory. All his pious 
friends encouraged him to join the church, which he 
did soon after. He then set about his Master’s work 
with joy. We had a delightful revival in the church 
and congregation. He soon collected together from 
twenty to thirty young persons, saying, ‘Come with 
us, and our God will surely do you good.’ O how 
many delightful meetings were held for prayer and 
conversation with our dear minister! My dear brother 
was made useful to the conversion of souls at that time. 
Would to God we could see many such young con- 
verts laboring with ardor in the same good cause.” 
Thus far is his sister’s report. Some time after this 
period, his mind became impressed with the duty of 
preaching to others that Gospel which he had found 
to be the power of God unto the salvation of his own 
soul. It bore on his mind with so much weight that 
his health was affected. He had the most solemn ideas 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 9 


of the responsibility of the sacred office, and the ne- 
cessity of a clear call from God. After many months 
spent in strong cries and earnest wrestlings with God, 
his feelings on the subject were so intense, that he has 
been heard to say, he lay prostrate under some trees, 
and, with feelings which none but God could compre- 
hend, agonized in prayer, that the impressions on his 
mind relative to the ministry might be removed; but 
the answer appeared to be, “ Wo is me, if I preach not 
the Gospel; a dispensation of the Gospel is committed 
unto me.” At length, with deep humility and self- 
abhorrence, relying on the Savior’s strength, he per- 
mitted his mind to dwell on the subject, and could 
view it with calmness. His desire for the salvation of 
souls was intense; he longed, he wrestled, for their 
salvation. The church encouraged him, and he was 
invited to exercise his gifts for the ministry. The una- 
nimous opinion of the minister and church decided 
that it was his duty to proceed. 

In another memorandum he says, “ The first time I 
ever attempted to open a text was to a poor villager. 
I preached about three minutes from Matt. 26 : 40.” 

“ At a prayer-meeting at Elstow I attempted for the 
first time to speak in public, from ‘ Come and hear, all 
ye that fear God, and I will declare what he hath done 
for my soul.’ Here the great and blessed God employed 
me as an instrument in the conversion of an old man, 
who gave decided evidence of a change of heart.” 

Thus encouraged, Mr. Kilpin willingly consented to 
enter the Bristol Academy, when about nineteen years 
old. His conduct there was marked by diligence, zeal, 
and piety. He gained the confidence and esteem of his 
beloved and revered tutor, Dr. Ryland, who perceived 
in him those earnest longings for the salvation of souls, 


10 LIFE OF THE 


whick promised great usefulness in the vineyard of 
his God. i 

After five years residence with Dr. Ryland, Mr. 
Kilpin received an invitation from the Baptist church 
at Leominster to become their minister. 

The church and congregation were few in number. 
Every method that could be devised appears to have 
been employed by the young minister to enlarge his 
boundaries, and he was blessed to the conversion of 
many souls, but his extensive views and desires aimed 
at greater usefulness. 

Soon after the commencement of his ministerial la - 
bors, several apparently trivial circumstances gave an 
entire change to his academic style of preaching. He 
was in the habit of studying closely, and writing near- 
ly the whole of his sermons, to which he made refer- 
ence during the time of their delivery. Thus furnished, 
he visited some part of Wales. Immense congrega- 
tions assembled. He was told that they did not like 
any but extemporaneous preaching. For this he was 
not prepared; but by condensing his subject he less- 
ened the appearance of his notes. With a trembling 
hand he placed his remarks in the Bible so neatly that 
his fears vanished. This, however, was followed by a 
hard cough, which, to his dismay, released the con- 
cealed paper, and sent it into a current of air, where it 
floated as a kite over the heads of the people. He 
breathed a momentary prayer for aid, when with good 
humor he was enabled to say, “So let Dagon fall, we 
will endeavor to seek higher assistance.” This pleased 
the congregation, who almost shouted with joy. He 
was enabled calmly to collect his thoughts, and the 
sermon was made a blessing. 

Although he would have deemed it an insult to offer 


REV. SAMUEL. KILPIN. ll 


to God what had cost him nothing, yet he acted often 
on the recollection of this event, as a proof that, should 
the providence of God bring him into contact with im- 
mortal souls, he ought not to lose the opportunity of 
attempting to gain some from the consideration that 
he was not prepared. When God calls to duty, he 
qualifies for the right performance. 

At another time whilst preaching, but not in his own 
pulpit, he mentioned the great God by the name of 
“the Deity.” A sailor, who was listening, immediate- 
ly started from his seat, his elbows fully spread, and 
exclaimed aloud, ‘‘ Deity—well, who is He? is He our 
God Almighty?” The attendants were about to turn 
him out ; but the minister stood reproved, and requested 
him to resume his seat, with the remark, “ Yes, my 
friend, I did mean the Almighty God.” The sailor re- 
joined, “I thought so, but was not quite sure—I never 
heard that name before.” ‘The humbled minister re- 
plied, “ You had aright to inquire; I was to blame; 
whilst delivering God’s message of mercy and justice 
to immortal souls, I ought not to have given my divine 
Master a name which prevented the message from be- 
ing understood.” “ Thank you, sir,” was the sailor’s 
reply ; and he looked as though he would have devour- 
ed the remaining part of the sermon. After the service, 
he came and begged pardon for the interruption, and 
with a sailor’s frankness requested the kind gentleman 
to take some refreshment with him, to make it up. 

This incident led Mr. Kilpin to very serious reflec- 
tions relative to his important office. He stood as an 
ambassador for Christ, entreating his congregation, in 
Christ’s stead, “Be ye reconciled unto God.” He stood 
between the living God and dead sinners, who were on 
the verge of death and hell. He thought that one ser- 


12 LIFE OF THE 


mon might be the means of rescuing souls from eternal 
misery, or might prove a savor of death unto death. 
What an awful and responsible situation! Here is a 
poor sailor, with scarcely any means of information 
relative to the importance of his soul; he is interested 
in the subject, when, by employing language which he 
does not understand, his mind is taken from awful 
realities, and fixed on the meaning of words. Alas, alas! 
how did the Savior act? He so preached that the com- 
mon people heard him gladly. Was this for want of 
the knowledge of higher language, or a superior style, 
or from ignorance? No, from neither, but from a know-. 
ledge of the value of the soul—the love of souls. 

From this period our young minister was deter- 
mined, in the strength of God, to lay every talent at 
the foot of the cross; and he deemed it an infinitely su- 
perior honor to be employed by the Holy Ghost in 
the conversion of a poor sailor, than to have obtained 
the mere applauses of the universe. On this sentiment 
he acted until death closed his services. 

When about to preach before ministers or others, 
upon public occasions, he wrote on the top of his card 
or notes, “‘ Present the Lord Jesus Christ, fear Him, 
and love the people.” 

On one occasion, when from home, he preached on 
the divinity of Christ, and brought into view many 
objections, that he might oppose them. He felt more 
than usual complacency in the strength of his argu- 
ments, but one of the people followed him to the 
vestry, and thanked him for the sermon, remarking, 
“Tt was a good discourse; but there was not one in 
the congregation that doubted the divinity of the Sa- 
vior.” His devout mind was roused on reflecting that 
he had assembled the people, and instead of feeding 


REV. SAMUEL-KILPIN. 13 


the flock of Christ with the bread of life, he had been 
needlessly choking them with the sour grapes of con- 
troversy, and had, perhaps. excited in the minds of 
some unconvertec, unawakened sinners, doubts and 
difficulties, of which before they had no idea. He ever 
after preached Christ crucified, not as a controversialist, 
but as one anxious to win souls for his kingdom. 

Mr. Kilpin exerted his full strength for the salvation 
of the villagers around him. He regularly preached 
weekly lectures, amidst persecution and opposition in 
various forms, at the hazard of his life. In one in- 
stance, a party, who were wrongly styled “ gentle- 
men,” unchained a vicious mastiffdog, and leading 
him to the preaching-room, fiercely directed him to 
attack the preacher, who was standing on the floor. 
The dog entered, apparently with the intention of obey- 
ing its master’s orders, but the mild good-humored 
eountenance and voice of the preacher arrested him; 
and when the poor creature was patted on the neck, 
and told to lie still, he peacefully took his station at 
the feet of his new friend, and no one could remove 
him, until the service was concluded. This was a se- 
vere reproof to his cruel masters. 

Another time, on returning home, a group of men 
sent a bull-dog to attack the mouth of his horse, and, 
had not the animal, by a sudden plunge, extricated 
himself and his rider from this danger, serious injury 
to both would probably have ensued. Eggs, stones, 
sticks, and various missiles, with the music of brass 
pans, frying-pans, tongs, &c. often surrounded him ;; 
Mr. Kilpin’s pleasant cheerful mind and manner help-~ 
ed him through many difficulties of this kind, and pre- 
vented much mischief. These labors were continued 


until he left the neighborhood. He was the active pio- 
2 Kilpin. 


14 LIFE OF THE 


neer, and succeeding laborers have reaped advantage 
from his exertions. 

Mr. Kilpin had usually several students in his house, 
and sometimes two or three sons of neighboring gen- 
tlemen, to whose education he attended. Most of the 
young men have proved that they partook of their 
tutor’s spirit, and are successful laborers in the Lord’s 
vineyard. Several of the youths have filled important 
stations, and retained the most child-like affection for 
him. 

Soon after his ordination and settlement as a pastor, 
he married Miss Wyke, of Eton, near Leominster, by 
whom he had four children; three died in their infan- 
cy. He knew what domestic, personal, and congrega- 
tional troubles were, from painful experience; but, 
under all these, the interests of the church of Christ 
bore most heavily on his heart. 

The circumstances attending the birth and removal 
of his three eldest children he narrated as follows, some 
years after, in a letter to a friend: 

“You are not, as I am, written childless. After my 
first marriage I was uncommonly anxious to have 
children. Thad four. The first only just lived to gasp, 
and died! I was not like a man, but more like a fool 
under this loss for some time, and very unlike a Chris- 
tian. On the fourth day, while weeping and lamenting 
my calamity, this portion of Scripture, 1 Sam. 1: 8, 
struck my mind: ‘Why weepest thou? why eatest 
thou not? and why is thy heart grieved? Am not I 
better to thee than ten sons?’ I bowed, and came to 
my senses. Our Lord forgave me. I preached from 
the text the next day, and never, no never, felt a re~ 
pining thought afterward. In less than a year, the 
Lord gave us a sweet little daughter, her face and form 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 15 


beautiful, but lame and diseased in her feet. She died 
in less than a month. I thought this would have caus- 
ed the mother’s death, but I had a sweet view of Job, 
1: 21, ‘The Lord gave,’ &c., and never repined. In 
about a year after, we had another daughter ; she lived 


. to run about and prattle, to our great delight. One day 


she ran to the parlor-door to admit a dear friend who 
doted upon her. He, opening it at the same moment, 
knocked her backward. The fall injured her spine, 
and death ensued some time after. I held her whilea 
lancet was passed into her nearly three inches. She 
embraced me, crying out, O papa! O papa! The re- 
medies used to remove the effects of the injury were 
not successful. The next day I saw her countenance 
change. I carried her up stairs and laid her on the 
bed. She pushed me away, turned her face from me, 
anddied! This nearly overset us both ; my dear wife 
never got over it. I preached from 1 Sam. 3: 18, ‘It 
is the Lord, let him do what seemeth him good.’ I 
have often wept, but I was enabled not to murmur. 
These things made me an old man in experimental 
preaching ; these trials did me more real good than all 
my studies, and all my mercies. Two years after, my 
dear, dear Samuel was born. He was given in answer 
to prayer, and always enjoyed as such.” Some further 
particulars respecting this son will claim attention 


hereafter. 


In the town of Leominster Mr. Kilpin lived four- 
teen years, amidst evil report and good report. Bui he 
adored his God for that grace which prevented, at any 
period of his life, an attack on his moral or religious 
character. There he ever stood on firm ground ; his 
conduct could not be impeached. 

One day, passing along the street, he heard some 


16 THE LIFE OF 


persons say, “ There goes agood man; if ever any on 
went to heaven, he will go.” Self-complacency crep 
in, and he hoped that he should prevail on his neighbor 
to accompany him thither. Having to pass anothe 
_ street, a different class remarked, “If ever any on 
» deserved to be hanged, that man does ; he 1s trying to 
make every one mad.” Down came all self-approba~ 
tion, and he entered his house nearly with the same 
views as he had left it. : 

In addition to his other arduous labors, Mr. Kilpin” 
published sixteen tracts, which had a wide circulation. 
Several persons of his congregation were offended at 
some of their contents, although no names were men- 
tioned, or reference made to any one. At length Mr. 
Eilpin's mind was so exceedingly pained, from various. ; 
causes, that he resigned his pastoral office. 

One of the tracts just mentioned reached Exeter, in 
which city he was before unknown, either personally§ 
or by report. Although the tract was without name, 
either of person or place, yet some remarkable pro- 
vidential circumstances led to the discovery; and as a 
pastor was heeded for a church in that place, an invi- 
tati nm was sent to him. This was one of the events, in 
Divine Providence, which gave a character to the 
future events of his life. The invitation was accepted 
as a supply for a few weeks. The awful state of a 
large population weighed heavily on Mr. Kilpin’s soul, 
and induced him to decide on settling amongst ‘Kent 
The church was in a very low state. Labor, of the 
most abundant and varied description, appeared to 
await him. 

In a memorandum he says—“ On October 25, 1812, 
I preached my first sermon at Exeter, from the oe 
‘Tam determined to know nothing among you, save 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 17 


Jesus Christ and him crucified” The blessed God 
honored this first proclamation by the saving conver- 
sion of one soul, who for fifteen years has glorified 
God by active labors in his service. She is now a 
‘mother in Israel.” 

Shortly after, Mr. Kilpin’s family joined him; and 
as soon as he became acquainted with the general 
state of the city, he determined on residing in a part 
which was considered to include some of the worst of 
its inhabitants. The congregation committed to his 
care meanwhile increased rapidly, and his labors were 
unremitting. 

On engaging a house in the most neglected part of 
the city, he had the kitchen licensed, and fastened a 
notice on his front gate—“ Reading, and morning 
prayers, from eight o’clock unti! half-past eight every 
morning. Neighbors of all classes are respectfully 
invited to attend. Preaching every Wednesday even- 
ing.” 

On the next morning a large placard covered this 
notice, drawn up in profane and blasphemous terms, 
and personally directed against himself. 

This sight brought his friends around him with en- 
treaties to desist; while mockery in all its varied forms 
assailed him. But his steady soul increased its ener- 
gies, instead of being dismayed. He procured licenses 
for two or three other rooms, remarking that “the 
devil was alarmed for his kingdom.” But crafty as 
that fiend was, he had mistaken his character, and shot 
beyond the mark of policy. This gave publicity to the 
station, and increased the attendance, which became 
very numerous, from various motives. But many souls 
were converted to God, and their improvement of 


conduct was so evident, that some of the neighbors 
K 2% 


18 LIFE OF THE 


saw, acknowledged, and hated the change. Many of 
those who came to mock, went home to pray. 

Mr. Kilpin had an uncommon attachment to chil- 
dren, and a very singular talent for conveying instruc-— 
tions to their young minds. He was almost idolized 
by the children of his flock, and the youthful groups 
in the neighborhood formed his body guard, for he had — 
generally a company around him, when at home or 
abroad, in the house or the street. He loved them for — 
the Savior’s sake. Before long, prejudice began to — 
disappear; hard, unfeeling hearts among the parents 
were softened into kindness, and the most marked 
respect was shown to him. 

About this time the spirit of Antinomianism, with — 
all its direful attendants, awfully prevailed in that 
neighborhood. Harmony fled from among Mr. Kilpin’ Ss” 
charge. Their grieved pastor was unable to bear this, 
He tendered his resignation, and left the city forasea — 
son, in search of another spot on which to fix his tent 
and preach beneath his Savior’s peaceful standard. 
Several invitations followed him from different places, 
and he returned to conduct his family from the scene 
of strife. His friends rallied, assumed courage, and 
gained a large majority, "bo solicited his return as 
their pastor. This he accepted, on condition that those © 
who were discontented would retire. 

Tu a short period their places were filled up, and all 
became regular and tranquil, when Mr. Kilpin resumed 
his delightful labors with increased success, and the 
mantle of love covered all past transactions, as far as 
his power extended. 

He resumed his morning devotional exercises: the 
congregations were very numerous, and far too crowded 
to be comfortable. He sought a larger house, which, 
when obtained, was yet too small. 


' 
] 
i 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 19 


His character as a man, a christian minister and 
pastor, as a husband, father, brother, master, friend 
and neighbor, are portrayed in his Jetters more faith- 
fully and vividly than they could be depicted by a 
lengthened detail. His confiding, frank, open-hearted- 
ness, appears in almost every line he wrote. All was 
simplicity; no studied precision; but the expressions 
flowed from his pen as the thoughts arose in his mind. 
The chief subject of his correspondence was his la- 
bors—the work in which his Master employed him; 
but the charge of egotism could not justly be founded 
upon his letters, any more than upon the words of 
David, when he said, “ Come, all ye that fear God, and 
I will tell you what he has done for my soul.” The 
language of Mr. Kilpin’s heart was not, “ Come and 
see my zeal for the Lord;” but, “see what God hath 
wrought ;” what the precious sacrifice of redeeming 
love has effected. He recorded the particulars of his 
own proceedings with as much or more impartiality 
than he would have exhibited in detailing the opera- 
tions of others. As the greater part of the memoran- 
dums and letters are without date, a precisely correct 
arrangement cannot be made, but they are placed ir 
succession as nearly as can be ascertained.*

is now regular; I am in health; and if I had a little less

“My own house-congregation yields precious sou

and God has honored it by filling it with precious souls.

Sometimes the views I have of the glory of God i 
Jesus Christ in the Gospel, connected with the im-

should not wonder if you were to hear I am suddenly 
gone into another world. It has cost me much self- 
denial to get into the habit' of living as I do; but I do 
not think [ should have stood or regained my ground 
at Exeter, if I had not. They cannot say I live too 
high, or that I go to see them that are rich, and not 
the poor. No, no; blessed be God, I am independent 
of this. Igo to them about their suuls and their sor-

ther place. Forty have been added, and next April we 
expect ten more.

Toa Friend. May 2, 1815. 


“Very HIGHLY RESPECTED FRIEND, 

“ Yours, in conjunction with that of my good friend 
the doctor, made me leap for joy. I exclaimed to my 
students, Now I will continue to live by faith upon our 
Lord, and I shall see a little chapel on my own pre- 
mises for the poor. Having thanked our dear Lord 
for his mercy, I ran down to the owner of the premi- 
ses. She said insultingly, that she was brought up to 
the church, and would not allow a farthing for a meet- 
ing-house. I was going to be angry, but recollected 
that He whom she had the most insulted, bore with 
her. Old Adam was getting up very fast; but the 
second Adam said, ‘ Vengeance is mine’—go to your 
work—let her alone. Here at present it rests, and I 
am non-suited for the moment; perhaps the enemy 
secretly will say, Where now is your God? If he does, 
Lhave an answer—Our God is in the heavens; the way 
will yet open, and appear to be of God; but this is the 
way the Lord deals with me; he opens, and out I dash 
to the work; again he shuts, to let me know he will 
employ me, but it is to be of him wholly. However, 
he gives me constant enjoyment, and an uninterrupted 
state of calm pleasure. In his presence I feel that I 
am his servant, and I know he sees that I love him, 
and only wait to ascertain his will. I feel his work is 
all my delight, the same as the merchant feels his em- 
ployment his delight. Ours is a heavenly merchan- 


— a 


22 LIFE OF THE 


dise, through grace. Before I dismiss this subject, . 
must remark one thing more. I have very sensibly 
found, that the more ardent my spirit is for his glory 
simply, the more I enjoy of personal interest in his sal- 
vation. .I used to say, if the Lord would but relieve 
me, and give me to see my interest clear in his love, 
then I would indeed devote myself to his glory. But 
seeking his glory, and leaving him to make me feel 
appropriating grace, I have found my own life by lo- 
sing it abundantly; and through great mercy, for it is 
all of mercy, I have no doubts whatever. Concerning 
our meetings relative to itineracy, we have held two. 
I preached from Isaiah, ‘ He shall not fail, nor be dis- 
couraged, till he have set judgment in the earth, and 
the isles shall wait for his law.’ My object was to 
show the glory of the Savior’s work, and the certainty 
of its being accomplished, and by way of inference, 
that we are justified in attempting any thing for his 
glory, for he will certainly bless us. And, until the 
whole earth is actually filled with his Gospel, we 
ought not to slacken our hands or prayers. After the 
sermon, some persons remarked, they knew human 
nature, and were sure it could not be accomplished. 
My soul said, ‘I care nothing about human nature; 
the more powerful it is, the more need have we to 
spread the blessings of Calvary. Grace from Christ, 
and in his name is our strength.’ Now for my dear 
children. I have been almost worn out by labor, and 
, forced to lay by for a few days. The precious lambs 
came, but the doors were shut. They came the next 
morning, but sad news, Mr. Kilpin was worse. Some 
of them went home crying to their parents, saying, 
‘Now we shall have no one to pray for us; we shall 
have no prayers.’ Perhaps that expression will do 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 23 


more good than many sermons. Through mercy Iam 
now better. I rode forty miles last week, and held dif- 
ferent meetings; preached three times yesterday, and 
walked four and a half miles; and to-day feel improved, 
except a little sweet lassitude: to be weary in the ser- 
vice, but not of it, is delightful. The tracts I keep in 
constant motion, and have plenty at present. - I went 
to see a poor woman confined to her bed. She began, 
‘Here I am, sir; I have been praying all day: I cannot 
tell how it is, but since I have read the tracts, and been 
at your house, it seems as if my own works were good 
for nothing. I used to think, if I did as well as I could, 
that was all; but now I feel quite different.’ This gave 
me an opportunity of showing her the way of the 
Lord more perfectly. ‘I have two agents that lend and 
talk about tracts, one in the city, and one in the coun- 
try. Concerning the villages, I have no doubt but 
much good might be done. I have taken a place se- 
ven miles distant, at seven pounds per annum. The 
people say they can give the minister a bed and some 
refreshment, but cannot raise twenty shillings. Seve- 
rai other villages are in prospect, where I mien to 
establish Sabbath-schools.” 


To the same. May 12th, 1817. 


“Indeed, dear sir, it would have been a high grati- 
fication to have had your company with us. Such re- 
pasts are luxuries of the highest order. Iam quite of 
your opinion. I would not have you lock up your pe- 
cuniary benevolence in one place, however desirable ; _ 
for, by distributing it, you keep things alive. Perhaps 
it would be an advantage to the cause, and to myself, 
to say, Here will I live, O Lord, and die in thy service, 
and trust in thee to raise up friends as they may be 
wanted. 


24 ’ LIFE OF THE . * 


“If I do not obtain a chapel here, I must worship in 
the yard. Last night my house was literally crammed, 
and one side of the house will not hold the merning 
congregation ; seven long forms will not seat the peo- 
ple and children. One little child, very little, was 
heard on her knees praying, ‘ Dear Lord, send Mr. Kil- 
pin back; don’t let him go away from us; Lord bless 
him.” The poor people absolutely prayed me back 
again to my place; and notwithstanding we have dis- 
missed a great many, (see page 18,) yet our congrega- 
tion is again as large as the chapel will contain. We 
have more at prayer-meetings, and I have had more, 
many more, at my house, for the season of the year, 
than usual. , 

“TI cannot give you any very particular account 
about children, except of one who died suddenly 
whilst I was away; he was only ill five hours. This 
little fellow was a constant attendant at morning 
prayers, however boisterous the weather. About eight 
months ago his shoes were worn out, and it was the 
Lord’s day. His mother would not let him come. He 
begged that he might. She said, ‘You cannot, you 
have no shoes.’ ‘Shoes, mother! said he with eager- 
ness ; ‘the great God don’t look at shoes; it is the heart, 
Mr. Kilpin says.’ He took his old shoes, and ran away 
to join us. About two months ago he awoke about 
one o’clock, saying, ‘Mother, O mother, I forgot to say 
my prayers; what shallI do? ‘Lie still, my dear, and 
(S2Y them in bed ; it is quite dark,’ ‘What! say prayers 
‘in bed; that is lazy work, Mr. Kilpin says” Out he 
sprang on the floor, and prayed. About five days be- 
fore he died, when in perfect health, he said, ‘Mother, 
do you think I shall go to heaven when I die? ‘Ihope 
so, my dear.” ‘Sodo!,saidhe. ‘Yes, my deat but 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 25 


I hope you will not die yet” ‘You cannot tell that,’ 
he replied, and looked very serious. ‘Three or four 
days after, he came from school at five o’clock, said he 
was sick, lay down, and was dead at ten that evening. 
Remarkable health has in general attended the chil- 
dren ; and where it has been wanting, a little help from 
my medicine-chest has usually set them right. Seve- 
ral are under serious impressions. 

“But, my dear sir, I can tell you something else. 
Calling them together in the morning, and sending 
home tracts and books, has had great effect on the pa- 
rents. A man and his wife came publicly before the 
‘church, and gave in their experience, which would 
have greatly affected you. ‘We, sir, never cared about 
our souls; had no thought of them, until our children 
came to our house, and told us what you said, and we 
read the sweet books. My husband left me one Sun- 
day night, and when he came back, he said, I have 
been hearing Mr. Kilpin; you cannot think what a 
kind man he is; he spoke to me; I never heard such 
things before; I'll go again.’ He did so, and did not 
know what to make of it, but said, We are wrong. O 
no, she replied, we are as good as our neighbors. Ah, 
but we have neglected the souls of the children! O, 
pooh! was the reply. Sabbath night returned again. 
She said, I should like to hear the man, if you will re- 
main with the children. She returned home more 
deeply affected than he had been. From that time, now 
a year and a half since, they come constantly, with all 
their children, to the house of God, and have prevailed 
upon their aged father, who comes, and is under very 
serious impressions. These two persons never heard 
a hint of religion before. They sat down with us at 


the Lord’s table, with six others, who were baptized in

the morning. This, to me, is a soul-cheering reward

“Next to our dear Lord, they will, in eternity, and

have stood my ground but for you, humanly speaking. 
My own resources I had spent in the same way, and 
had no means of recruiting, but was ready to give up 
all in despair. Then the great God sent you, and I 
was ready to exclaim, What has God wrought! 

‘“‘May the Savior spare your valuable benevolent life, 
with the power cf thus serving Him that loved you, 
and gave you the power and the heart to help in the 
cause of souls. I need not endeavor to present future 
reward to animate your mind to a continuance in well- 
‘doing. To hear of events so important is a happiness 
that none know but those who are close imitators of 
Him that loved them, and gave himself forthem. Yet 
the Lord has said, we shall not give a cup of cold wa- 
ter without receiving areward. I envy you the ability 
thus to disseminate blessings, whilst, with unspeakable 
delight, I adore Him that uses me in his hands to make 
known the savor of his dear name. Pray our dear 
Lord for me, that I may never get into business. Plead 
for me that the Lord would open a way that I may 
continue in my present place, and not leave this neigh- 
borhood, but that I may go on in the strength of the

“Concerning villages around, I eagerly pushed and 
moved every thing that was moveable, and called upon 
friends to help. I offered to be bound, and am now 
bound for three houses; the rent of them amounts to 


REY. SAMUEL KILPIN. 27 


£17 per annum. I am safe as it respects one; a se- 
cond I have some fears about, but not many; for the 
third I must look out for supplies; but I do not wish 
you to help me with your great kindness: I think I 
can manage this branch. But I have none to help’ me 
with my children but yourself. I shall never forget a 
good woman, a praying, visiting woman, calling to see 
the mother of a sick child, that was really dying. Af- 
ter she had coldly looked upon the little creature, the 
poor mother said, ‘Will you pray with my daugh- 
ter? She replied at the door, ‘No, no, it is only a child.’ 
The little creature pushed aside the bed-clothes, and 
exclaimed, with all her remaining strength, ‘Yes, Iam 
a child, but I have a soul!’ What a reproof! What 
will He think of such persons, who said, ‘Suffer little 
children to come unto me, and forbid them not? That 
dear Lord wept over Jerusalem. Once, He was ‘much 
displeased’—not with those who spit in his face, nor 
with those who mocked him, nor those who plucked 
out the hair, nor with Peter who denied him, nor Judas 
who betrayed him, nor Pilate who condemned him, nor 
the Jews who crucified him; but with his disciples, be- 
cause they would not let children be brought to him: 
‘Forbid them not,’ he said. Once in spirit he rejoiced, 
but at what? That the devils were subject to him ? 
No. That angels attended him? No; but at the ho- 
sannas of children! ‘Hearest thou what these say ? 
Yes, he said, ‘It is written, Out of the mouths of babes 
and sucklings thou hast perfected praise.’ 

“My house on Sabbath morning was filled with pre- 
cious souls. My prayers are more and more ardent 
about a chapel, of the description I mentioned, in this 
part of the city; the more the difficulties arise, the 
more the plan seems eligible and necessary ; the Lord

shall open a way himself. Dear sir, lend the assistance

but by prayer and fasting. Sweet words! I must try 
them. Mark, 9:29.. There are things that must have 
extraordinary exertions made to obtain them, not to 
make Him willing, but they will not be sent in any 
other way.

To thesame. April 8, 1818. 
“Very DEAR sin,—I have just received information

you will excuse haste. I could not so cordially, so 
willingly receive your fruits of grace, were I not con- 
scious of two things: First, I deny myself those things 
that the generality of professors think, and think cor- 
rectly, they are justified in enjoying. Secondly, I need

not be under any pecuniary obligations, if I used the 
income which I have, for my own and family’s servi-

from the extra expenses of my situation, and labors 
among the poor and miserable. The garret of mise-

the hand and heart must be stretched out. When I

medicine is suggested, and they must have it. While 
Isee God our Father blessing, I must continue. When 
‘ Fsee the soul brought under religious feelings, and af- 
terward attending the house of God, I must continue. 
My friends have said, ‘You ought not to make your- 
self anxious, and be short of money, often without a 
shilling, when you have made your domestic expenses 
come within your income. If you could keep that to 
yourselves, you are as independent as he that hasa 
thousand a-year. It is not your duty to perplex your- 
self. However plausible all this may be, my answer 
to all such that trouble me is, ‘When the Lord ceaseth 
to bless the means thus used for souls as well as bo- 
dies, and will not make up the deficiency for one year, 
then I will give up to this mode of right natural rea- 
soning. But as long as he raises assistance, and sanc- 
tions the work by his very especial blessing, I must 
pursue my present plan, distressed as I often am.’ I 
want to raise great-coats and hats for forty boys, and 
cloaks and bonnets for forty girls, but I am afraid that 
is beyond all bounds. You shall have my plan when 
it is matured, and any suggestions will be esteemed. 
“Now for an incident. I think I sent you word that 
a dear little boy said to Betty, ‘Will God Almighty 
live at the new house?’ meaning, would there be morn- 
ing prayers. This idea a God Almighty living at a

30 LIFE OF THE 


house, being, in his mind, synonymous to morning 
prayers, very much affected me. Since then, the poor 
little fellow has been very ill of the small-pox ; he sent 
to say he could not come. ‘There,’ said he, weeping 
in bed, ‘the bell rings, and I cannot go!’ I sent word 
I would go to see him, but understanding the eruption 
was coming out, I put it off till the next day. AsI 
was then getting up from dinner to go, the bell rang, 
and who should be at the door, wrapped in a cloak, but 
the dear boy; his mother was obliged to bring him out 
of bed to be prayed for! We kneeled down together 
in the study—his mother knew no more about religion 
than a brute—but who can tell the result? He came 
the next morning, and he is now well. 

“ Another incident. Not being accustomed to spend 
many days without hearing some fresh instance of con- 
version—(wonderful grace !)—-andhaving received trou- 
bles and insults from some persons who hate religion, 
and never being accustomed to live any where without 
friendship, my pride was hurt, and my spirits became 
at one time a little irritated. But I gave it all up to 
to God, and doubled my diligence. The next Sabbath 
morning a poor old father was brought to morning 
prayer by his three children: he has been on Wednes- 
day evenings and Sabbath mornings ever since. He 
looks and wonders, and begs tracts—the end we wait 
for: but yesterday, going amongst the poor, one said, 
‘J wish you had been here a little sooner ; poor A—— 
was here, and another, sir, whom the Lord has called 
at the new house.’ O, thought I, the reason of the. 
devil’s roaring is explained. They now walk more 
than a mile three times on the Sabbath, and are regu- 
lar on Wednesday nights. Another poor vile sinner 
ealled and said, ‘Sir, but what is a man to do that can

not pray? Ah, sir, I have been—I have been,’ &c,— 
Well, there is mercy even for him! Pray for me and 
mine; I do for you and yours. My house will-not 
hold the people.” 


To thesame. Oct. 21, 1818. 


“My pear sir,—Your kindness, with that of your 
friends, and the general appearing of the Lord my God 
for me, has almost overpowered my feelings. I can 
do little else but bless and adore the Lord; I feel the 
kindness particularly of yourself and friends. I ven- 
tured to commit that part which had reference to my- 
self into the hands of God and my friends, and he and 
they have worked together for me. It would have been 
asore trial to have accomplished it at my own expense, 
and to have been burdened with a dead weight. But 
why do I thus write? the Lord is my God, and though 
he will suffer, or rather honor some persons to lose 
their life for him, they shall assuredly find a better. 
About six months agoa very respectable young person 
ealled one day, and said she should like to have some 
tracts. I gave her some; she returned them in two or 
three days, but unread. I offered more, with this re- 
mark, ‘ You must pray over them, or they will do you 
no good. ‘I shall not pray,’ she said to herself, ‘I 
never did such a thing;’ but still the words, ‘You must 
pray,’ remained. The end of the tale is, she is now, 
and has been for these four months, a praying person. 
To-day she told me ‘all things were new’ to her—a 
new Bible, new company, new feelings, new desires; 
adding, ‘Thad been Jost, but now through mercy I hope 
in our Lord Jesus Christ.’ This is the blessed effect 
of tracts. Does not this reward for all the expense 
and trouble? Does not this justify the practice of 


32 LIFE OF THE 


dedicating them first unto the Lord, before they are 
distributed? To him be all the glory! The Lord 
look upon you, dear sir, and preserve you from losses, 
and give you the gains a thousand fold. Pray for me, 
dear sir.” 


To another friend. Sept. 5, 1818. 


“When we act purely, and think so, then we are 
most like Christians, and so most like Christ ; and then 
we say, ‘Not my will, but thine be done. We may 
think this in our judgment; we may say this with our 
lips, but we cannot feel it without grace. ‘Thy will 
be done,’ when our wills are contrary, can never be 
said and felt, except from a divine principle; but we 
may trembling say, ‘ Lord, remember we are but dust ;? 
we have no business to say, ‘Why doest thou so? it 
would look like arraigning him. But we may say, 
‘Show me wherefore thou contendest with me.’ It 
cannot be in wrath, since he has laid our iniquities on 
his beloved Son! It cannot be anger, because he ney- 
er knew that, for he is always love. It is altogether 
like a father’s best and wisest feelings, ‘ Like as a fa- 
ther pitieth his children.’ Well, we have had fathers 
who have corrected us, as they have thought, properly, 
but He always for our profit. He has no other end in 
view ; it is for us and for our benefit. Dear brother, 
do not let the enemy creep in and suggest that correc- 
tion is not out of love, and not for your benefit. It is 
not some strange, mysterious, half-bad plan, but the 
very best that infinite wisdom and covenant-love can | 
prescribe. Everv drop in that cup is weighed and | 
measured.” 


, REY. SAMUEL KILPIN. , 38 


To Mrs. —. 


“How agree the waters of affliction with you? are 
they bitter? They may be so; but they are whole- 
some. Throw in a little piece of the Tree from Cal- 
vary, and it will instantly make the waters sweet, 
strengthening, and bracing. 


There is a wonder-working Wood, 
I’ve heard believers say, 

Can make these bitter waters good, 
And take the curse away. 


The murmurings of the children of Israel about the 
waters were much worse than the waters, infinitely 
more bitter. O whata mercy to the body, in its deep- 
est affiictions, to have a humble thankful soul! Ingrati- 
tude is the worst of all sins. Even that monster of 
ingratitude, Absalom, while in pursuit of his father’s 
blood, could say to Hushai, ‘Is this thy kindness to 
thy friend? We enjoy no more than we are thank- 
ful for. We may have thousands of mercies; yes, we 
have ten thousand mercies, but not one is enjoyed ex- 
cept our eyes see the Giver, and our lips acknowledge 
His goodness. When the heart feels its Lord as the 
giver, how sweet is the hard dry crust of the poor man! 
And O, my beloved, when our hands take the bread 
and wine from our broken, bleeding Savior, whilst he 
kindly, just like himself, smiles and whispers, ‘ You are 
welcome, my beloved;’ how are our hearts charmed! 

“Now a line to my dear brother Joseph. Whata 
mercy that you have not been treated as Joseph of old 
was! The archers shot at him, but the hand of his 
arms was made strong by the God of Jacob. This 


34 LIFE OF THE 


latter part has been your experience. Your name 
signifies, to increase; and God has given you to in- 
crease; this is as he pleases, who worketh all things 
according to the counsel of his own will. But thanks 
be unto God, this is his will, even our sanctification— 
this is an unspeakable mercy. Iam not sure it is his 
will that I should continue in health; though a large 
portion is enjoyed at present, yet affliction and death 
I expect will visit in their turn. I do not know that it 
is his will that I should be rich. At present there is 
no appearance of it. But this I know is his will, that 
I should be sanctified. This is enough; this is the 
best will of my heavenly Father; for this he afflicts 
our heads, our hearts, and hands; then let him do, 
dear brother, what he pleases with you, and with me. 
I say, Amen, and leave room for you to sign the same. 
Farewell; and believe, what you know to be true, that 
I love you.” 


Toa Friend. August 20th, 1818. 


“My pear FRIEND— When I think of the workings of 
Providence, its progress, its influence, from our first 
operations, I am lost in wonder, love, and praise. I ven- 
tured to open my house for preaching, with much fear 
and trembling ; but when I think of the trophies of di- 
vine grace, and the moral improvement of this de- 
praved neighborhood, and what is still more, of the con- 
version of at least thirty precious souls to God, most 
of whom have made a public profession of religion, 
and have maintained their character; add to this the 
happy end of many dear children, one recently, and 
her death the spiritual life of a grown-up person; and 
_ to this add the increase of daily attendance, so that we 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 35 


have not room for the people and children in the mort 
ing; I exclaim, ‘What hath God wrought!’ Iam again 
gently warned, that I am not to continue in my present 
place; but the Lord has seen my steps, and has opened 
a way into a large garden, where there are some skele- 
ton premises. I have taken these premises on a lease 
for eighteen years, for £21 per annum. My further 
plan is, to establish two Sunday-schools, and to make 
it a kind of depot, or rendezvous for Sunday-school 
children. I sometimes weep and tremble with fear, 
and have said to myself, Make out your bill, and lay it 
before the Lord. I have done so, as Hezekiah did, 
and have cash in hand to begin with. In him I im- 
plicitly trust to accomplish his and my purposes. My 
dear Samuel grows a fine boy; is nearly as tall as his 
mother; all, trough mercy, in health; he begins to 
be very useful in the glorious work.—Farewell, yours 
verily.” 


The providence of God, in a manner truly wonder- 
ful, led to the possession of a garden, in which were 
some old sheds. It might be called “a place of pits,” 
and had been used for cleansing and drying leather. 
Here Mr. Kilpin erected a small chapel, fitted up a 
school-room, and a place for occasional worship, also 
a small cottage as a residence for himself and family. 
A chain of very remarkable providences directed every 
step in the progress of this work, and the means were 
supplied by degrees, a considerable part by those who 
once had opposed his plans. "i 

Mr. Kilpin’s son, a fine youth, was the assistant and 
the steaty companion of his father’s labors, sorrows, 
and enjoyments. With pleasure they anticipated the 
opening of the new chapel. They named it Little 


36 LIFE 'OF THE 


Bethel, and the dear child toiled with great joy through 
the labors of each day. 

When all seemed fixed, according to human appre- 
hension; when the building was reared, the garden 
cultivated, and the cottage waiting to greet the inmates 
—death entered! First the healthful, sprightly son 
was snatched away, then the beloved wife. Disease 
left the faithful servant deprived of strength. But the 
childless widower stood weeping and looking upward 
to Him who said, In me is your help found. 

In the account of his son, written by himself, and 
appended to this memoir, will be found some painfully 
interesting particulars relative to this bereavement. 

The son, Samuel Wyke Kilpin, died 4th December, 
1818; his mother on the 18th of the same month. 

Mr. Kilpin wrote, at the time, as follows: “Ah, my 
dear sir!—how shall I state my affliction? My dar- 
ling, darling child, Samuel, who helped to build the 
chapel, and was to have taken a very active part in 
that work, is taken away! Death came, and would 
not go without him. -I wept and prayed—but no, he 
would not hear. His poor mother—alas! the wound 
was so severe she could not sustain it. Death struck 
her also; and now, dear sir, she lies dead! I only am 
left alone to tell the tale of wo. My heart at times 
swells largely. However, He that wept tells me I may 
weep because I loved them, But my mind is submis- 
sive; I would not bring them back again. Three 
things comfort me. I have no doubt of their safety; 
Ihave none of my own; and I am sure all is well, and 
just as*it should be. He does all things well. But, 
dearsir, O pray for me. I wish I could see you. F 
cannot write much.” 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 37 
In another letter to the same friend, he says— 


“My pear str—What shall I say to you? My dar- 
sing boy was to have given you a number of particu- 
vars; but how shall I write, when he, dear fellow, is 
not? It is my full intention to write the history of 
his conversion to God, which was very singular. My 
nerves are shattered, though I regularly preach; and 
when speaking of my heavenly Father’s conduct, I 
feel as if in heaven. My soul has been uniformly, 
through the whole, as though in heaven. All above 
has been as calm as possible; as a pure sea of glass, 
clear as crystal. I have never doubted the wisdom, or 
goodness, or love of God. Iadvre him, bless him, and 
magnify him. If asigh would reinstate them in my 
arms, I would not suffer it to escape. No, no; all is 
well. But Iam sorely cut up, sorely broken. The 
stroke is awful; it is as much as I can live under. 
Pray, pray for me; my heart is wounded. 

“The chapel was opened on Christmas day (1818.) 
The Lord enabled me to give.a history of its rise and 
progress, end and design. I preached from Psalm 126: 
6. ‘He that goeth forth weeping, bearing precious 
seed, shall doubtless come again with rejoicing, bring- 
ing his sheaves with him.’ I remarked that these 
words were not only sacred to me, from being the 
words of God, but also from their being the words 
which the blessed God used for the conversion of him 
who this day was to have been one of the principal 
hearers, and whose voice was again to have charmed 
our ears. The words led to the following remarks: 

1. “No man shall ever attempt any thing for God 
without being blessed of him. He that goeth. forth 


with handfuls shall return with sheaves. nf 
] 4 Kilpin. 


$8 LIFE OF THE 


2. “That it is part of the determined counsel of 
Heaven, that no one shall succeed except he goeth forth 
weeping. When God ratified the covenant with Abra- 
ham, it was not until Isaac was upon the altar; then 
it was pronounced, ‘Now I know that thou fearest 
God, It was not until Joseph wept all the way from 
Canaan to Egypt, that the seed could be sown for the 
supply of his family, of Egypt, and the nations around. 
David could not sit firmly on his throne until he had 
been hunted upon the mountains. The handfuls of 
corn upon Calvary could not be spread abroad until 
the cross had borne the victim. The martyred disci- 
ples’ blood must bedew the earth before the apostles 
could sow the seed. The missionaries in the east must 
first weep over all their printing works consumed by 
fire, before the Bible could spread there. O my hear- 
ers, every step on these premises, in this house, I have 
watered with tears before I could open it, before the 
seed could be sown. He that sings now in heaven, 
might have been permitted to join us here; but no 
seed could be sown until his father had wept over every 
spot.—Ah, my dear sir, I cannot go on.—So this place 
of worship was opened. Tears have bedewed this 
scrawl. As soon as the clouds are somewhat gone I 
will write again.” 


After the loss of his wife and child, Mr. Kilpin was 
persuaded to visit his friends and relatives at Bedford 
and Newport Pagnel. The members of his family bore 
much affection for each other, and his wounded spirit 
found solace in their society. After sojourning with 
them for a short period, he returned to his work with 
-enewed health both of body and mind, as the follow: 
in letters show. 


REY, SAMUEL KILPIN. 38 


To Mr. J. P. C., Exeter.— Written at Bedford. 


“ Dear, VERY DEAR BroTuer,—My full intention isto 
be with you, God willing, on Tuesday evening, 27th, 
and I hope some dear friend and yourself will eat a 
crust with me in my new habitation. I suppose I shall 
have some painful pangs at first, but ‘the Lord can 
help.’ This is a saying that I am accustomed to find 
true. Thank you for the love of the people, and for 
your love; they are cordials to my heart. I know of 
no place, with all the extra affection that my lonely 
circumstances excited from every one, that I feel so 
decidedly about as Exeter. Exeter to me has every 
thing of this world. I expect to see greater things 
done there than I have yet seen. St. Thomas’s parish 
must yet bud and blossom. I expect the Lord means 
to reveal his arm. Where there are great siftings, and 
winnowings, and fannings, the chaff must go; for what 
is the chaff to the wheat? Pray for me. My love to 
all the people. I am now starting for London; the 
Lord help me to part with my friends ; but I feel like 
a poor fellow going to be transported. I preached my 
farewell sermon last night to a goodly number. I al- 
ways feel more whilst preaching in old Bunyan’s meet- 
ing than I do any where else. Pray for me, dear bro- 
ther. It isa mercy for me that I never longed to be 
at Exeter with my people so much as I do now.” 


To a Friend. April 28th, 1819. 


“ Money has no charms with me, only as I could 
distribute it for purposes of charity and benevolence. 
' I have engaged to give sixty bonnets and forty-eight 


40 LIFE OF THE 


hats by Whit-Monday. I must establish a daily school 
of industry for girls, which cannot be done under £30 
per annum expense, for the mistress must be respecta- 
ble. I shall not be satisfied until the same is done for 
boys. If you, dear sir, would allow me to depend on 
you, so that, if I cannot get any one to help, the work 
may proceed without them, you would be rendering 
the cause of God and of souls great service. I always 
perceive people will come to my help when they see I 
can get on without them: and, in general, to propose 
plans is merely to do nothing. Begin to build, and act 
determinately, and leave the work to recommend itself, 
and any thing will go on that is first dedicated to God 
in prayer. He will bless any thing for his glory, and 
the good of his creatures. 

“T have no books of any sort but those I send to you 
as specimens, and but very few tracts. I distributed 
1200 on my last journey into Bucks, Bedford, and 
Northampton. Through the different counties, pas- 
sengers within and outside the coaches requested them. 

“ Any assistance your heart may dictate, or any sug- 
gestion you may think proper to give, shall be con- 
scientiously attended to. My spirits are much better, 
but Tam often inundated. I need much your prayers; 
I am a poor lonely animal. When I go to my house, 
and sit in my study, and work, I am cheerful; but I 
have no fire-side company, no cheering wife, no en- 
chanting son. A faithful servant I have in poor Betty, 
but in my own house I have no society. 1 sit, and 
weep, and wail, and often bedew my bed with tears, 
until the Lord is pleased to administer unto the unwor- 
thy heir of salvation some comfort, then I drop asleep, 
Pray come on Whit-Monday, if you ean. I have ne 
temptations to offer you, but an affectionate heart, and 
a good lot of children in the schools, Pray for me.” 


“ REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 4h 


To the same. 


“Dear sir,—If the love of Christ does not influence, 
constrain us, bear us down like a torrent, much can- 
not be done, either for God or our fellow-creatures. 
Amidst such indifference and deadness, the objects one 
would wish to serve appear unwilling to receive the 
blessing. Moses had need of more patience to bear 
with the children of Israel, than with Pharaoh and all 
Egypt; they were more troublesome to him. Some- 
times I think, ‘If the people will perish, let them 
perish ;’ but then I think, ‘ Where had I been, if the 
Lord had treated me thus?’ He followed, pleaded, per- 
suaded, and at last plucked me as a brand from the 
burning. Thus must we proceed, expecting all from 
God; then shall we reap, if we faint not. If any thing 
is done in the villages here, it must be done free as air, 
more free than the running stream. I have felt ex- 
ceedingly unwell to-day; I could not assemble with 
the people ; I wept; it was solitary, yet comfortable, 
to think that the cause of God will go on when I am 
dead. I have bored through, and have been borne 
through, every difficulty for the past six years. The 
last words of my dear brother were, 


‘I see the sinking church arise, 
‘And leave the world in peace.’ 


“ My paper, my strength and spirits, tell me I must 
conclude.” ' 


Mr. Kilpin was always very anxious to promote the 
right observance of the Sabbath. In the neighborhood 


- where he resided in Exeter, he saw much of the dread- 
K a 


£2 LIFE OF THE 


ful effects resulting from the neglect of that holy day, 
and he used his best endeavors to check the torrent of 
iniquity. Exertions of this nature became the more 
needful, as some of those who have already been men- 
tioned as promoting the breach of harmony in this 
eliureh, both by precept and example discountenanced 
the due observance of the Sabbath. In Mr. Kilpin’s 
family the preparation commenced on Saturday even- 
ing, when the neighbors were invited to the little cha 
pel, that the mind might be relieved from worldly 
eares, and.prepared for the approaching festival. 

The following extract from a letter has reference to 
-the preaching of one of his opposers : 


“Last Wednesday night I heard Mr. preach 
that * * * *. Poor man! I fear he has never known 
_ at all the plague of his own heart. I know that I need 
no other sacrifice but Christ, and that he need not die 
again; and by his “one offering, he hath perfected for 
ever them that are sanctified;” but I, like you, do 
mourn, being burdened. I feel the need, not of a fresh 
Savior, but of fresh manifestations of that Savior. Low 
ground is best for sinners; but we must not look on 
the ground, ‘ Look up, and you will not fall,’ is a sen 
lence that often does me good. It was occasioned by 
a poor boy’s expression. He was in an orchard, upon 
sue of the trees, and his head became giddy. He cried 
it to his father, ‘Oh, I shall fall? ‘Look up,’ says 
une father, ‘and you will never fall” It has struck me 
forcibly many times since. Look up, and you will ne- 
ver fall. Look up at the cross of Christ, the Lamb of 
Cod; be all your hopes and mine in him; and then 
liis hold of us will be our security, and our hold of 
tin our comfort.”

The foliowing is a specimen of Mr. Kilpin’s Sabbath- 
cay labors in his Master’s service, about the same pe- 
riod as the preceding letters were written: 


“ Yesterday I was in my study by six o’clock; held 
a meeting at seven; prayed, read ‘hymns and a chap- 
ter, and exhorted for twenty minutes. At half-past ten 
walked to South-street, and preached a sermon. The 
Master sweat drops of blood in prayer, I only of water :* 
His were efficacious, mine only instrumental by his 
help. The subject: ‘Know your own selves.’ The 
place crowded, the people silent as death. Two o’clock 
—Preached, ‘ Examine yourselves, whether ye be in 
the faith.’ Administered the Lord’s supper. Dismissed 
the congregation, and held achurch meeting. Returned 
home, and preached at six to as full and as serious a 
congregation, ‘Christ is in you, except ye be repro- 
bates. Afterward had a private meeting with a poor 
deluded, tempted youth; and, thanks to God, felt at 
night as well as in the morning.” 


On deseending from the pulpit one Sabbath morn- 
ing, a stranger very politely requested him to dine with 
him at an inn. He replied, “ Dine with you, sir, at an 
‘inn in Exeter, on a Sabbath-day! No, sir, not if you 
would give me the city. A minister, who has to ad- 
dress souls on subjects connected with eternity, dine 
at an inn with company on the Sabbath-day! No, sir; 
except from necessity, I never sit with my family, but 
at a short meal, on the Sabbath. I have to preach to 
myself as well as to others. Excuse my firmness. I 
feel obliged by your kindness.” 

His little chapel now often exhibited most interest-

in full operation; and then if you will breathe the best 
air in our climate, come and see—look at us once.” 


Mr. Kilpin’s labors at this period are thus described 
in a letter to another friend, written in July, 1819: 


“You would be pleased with some things here; our 
friend was very much so. But the plants then 
were only budding. Since that time several have been 
in full bloom, and the Master, walking in his garden, 
admired them as the produce of his love and care. He 
. ordered them, nay, with his own hand he transplanted 
them into his kingdom above. O how has my heart 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN, 51 


been charmed with their artless breathings after God! 
The effect upon their parents, from lending and ex- 
changing the Tracts, has been very great. Our whole 
neighborhood wears a face as different as you can con- 
ceive. My morning services forced me out of my 
study into my hall, from thence into the dining-room, 
and another adjoining, and now into a little chapel. 
Yesterday I had service in it three times. A friend 
preached for me twice at the other chapel. That was 
full, and so was this, almost to suffocation. In the af- 
ternoon I rallied the schools, and in an hour or two we 
mustered near three hundred. At night the little chapel 
was full of souls, as solemn as death. I have now four- 
teen young people, as dear to me as my life, to whom 
I lend Tracts. They go to poor houses all over the 
city, to sick chambers, the hospitals, the asylums, and 
any where. They lend, and read, and pray; when 
they are asked for a Tract, they always give. To sup- 
port this, when I had money I spent it; when I had 
none, the Lord raised me up friends.” 


After stating his readiness to act gratuitously as 
agent for the Religious Tract Society, he proceeds, “T 
may perhaps be laughed at, but Ihave been accustomed 
to that. My morning service was the subject of ridi- 
eule, and—sneered ; but God has owned it to the con- 
version of souls, and the moral improvement of many 
hundreds. It will not be long ere I stand before the 
Judge of the whole earth, ‘the account to render there,’ 
so that the sneers of others have upon me the same 
effect as the whetstone upon the mower’s scythe be- 
fore my window.” 


The letter concluded with an urgent request for the 


52 LIFE OF THE 


person to whom it was addressed, and who was then 
unknown to Mr. Kilpin, to “ come and see.” He went. 
He saw in Mr. Kilpin’s little establishment what he 
never saw elsewhere, and what, perhaps, never was 
elsewhere to be seen. A friendship and correspond- 
ence between them ensued, which was uninterrupted 
till the death of the faithful servant of Christ. Amidst 
all that the visitor saw and admired in the proceedings 
of the devoted minister, he was much struck, both on 
that and on other occasions, by witnessing the almost 
magical influence of Mr. Kilpin over the groups of 
children that crowded around him. There ever ap- 
peared a peculiar and secret bond of affection and sym- 
pathy between him and even the youngest child with 
whom he had any intercourse. This was the more 
remarkable, as Mr. Kilpin possessed, and continually 
exercised, the power to make most energetic and alarm- 
ing appeals to the consciences of unawakened sinners. 


In another letter, written some time after, he says: 


“My pear Frienp,—The desire of my heart, a day 
school for the religious instruction of children, is esta- 
blished, and succeeds. My mind is so incessantly oc- 
cupied in the work of God, tnat little time remains for 
the luxury of indulged sorrow. 

“J have commenced the gratuitous depositary for 
the Tract Society, and have given a suitable room for 
the purpose. I have distributed ten thousand yearly, 
in various directions, before this depository was form- 
ed. The Lord bless this work also! The numerous 
children who continue to attend at morning prayers 
‘receive each a Tract, and exchange it the following

REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 53 


“Last Sabbath-day I preached a funeral sermon for 
a good old woman. She had been a member many 
years, but she never gave me the least pain by impro- 
per conduct: a great wonder in these days! She was 
very poor, but was a pillow to my head, and a pillar to 
my heart. 

“One of the most powerful wrestlers, and a great 
villain, came to meeting this evening, on purpose to 
get up a tale for a public-house; he returned home to 
pray! How gracious was the Lord to me, an ungrate- 
ful sinner! I will record my own weakness. I went 
to preach as an empty vessel, in such a frame that no 
one but my God would have borne with me. Thou 
knowest, O Lord, that I hated myself for it. 

“Some time ago I preached eight. discourses from 
Eph. 5. To my own soul they were delightful, but 
appeared to be entirely lost on the church. Except 
especially requested, I never intend to preach a set of 
sermons more, or to mention them as such. How 
humbling, that the subjects that cost me months of 
hard labor should be as water spilt upon arock! A 
few months before this I preached another coursc of 
lectures, which cost me months of hard study ; the con- 
gregation appeared quite uninterested, and I expect 
they did not retain one of them a week.” 


A person of Mr. Kilpin’s affectionate disposition 
could not long remain a solitary individual. His plans 
of usefulness, also, as noticed in the preceding letter, 
suffered much from the want of that female aid which 
could only be given by one united to him in the closest 
ties; and there were many works of charity and mercy 
from which he was now debarred. While thus situa- 
ted, his attention was directed to one in whom there 


K 5* 


54 LIFE OF THE 


was every appearance of his finding a suitable help- 
meet. He committed this matter to God with earnest 
prayer, as every other action of his life; the way be- 
came clear, and after a proper interval, he was united 
to one who shared his burden, and participated in his 
pleasures, during the rest of his life, in no ordinary 
degree.* Their union was blessed; they were spirits 
evidently calculated for each other ; and as no parental 
cares demanded her attention, Mrs. Kilpin was enabled 
to assist her husband in all his plans of benevolence. 
Many extracts from Mr. Kilpin’s letters might be given, 
showing the happiness he enjoyed in this union, and 
the thankfulness he ever expressed to Him by whose 
providence it was brought about; but as Mrs. Kilpin 
still survives her beloved partner, her feelings will be 
best consulted by allowing the subject to remain with- 
out further observation. It was indeed, in the fullest 
sense of the term, a happy wnion. She ever esteemed 
it a privilege to assist in his works of faith and labors 
of love, while he found in her a wise counsellor and 
affectionate supporter in the hour of trial; and in the 
painful time of sickness she attended him with an assi- 
duity and affection to which no description could do 
justice.

the year 1818 is cleared. O, to God how great a debtor 
Tam! I see many things that might be done, but I want
