have taken four pupils; but this wil] not do; my wife’s 
time, as well as my own, is consumed, and taken from 
pastoral concerns and pastoral visits. When I had 
students, they were in some measure assistants in my 
various pursuits. As it respects borrowing money, 
that 1 never, never will do: it would soon kill me. 
‘Owe no man any thing’ but love, saith the Lord.” 


To the same. June 28, 1821. 


“ Dear Sir,—! embrace an opportunity that presents 
itself of acknowledging in writing your last letter and 
enclosure, but I cannot find words that would be agree- 
able to you, that would at the same time express my 
feelings; therefore I would only say that it led me to 


56 LIFE OF THB 


that blessed Friend on whom we depend, and from 
whom all our mercies flow. By your kindness I am 
led to acts that lead to other actions, fearless of conse- 
quences, which would otherwise appall me. I am led 
to acts of faith, of industry, of benevolence. My prayers 
are encouraged, my God is adored, his cause is honored. 
The school of industry, by dint of the application of 
myself, and my better self, goes on well. We have 
now in daily attendance ninety girls and two gover- 
nesses, with a supply of Bibles, Testaments, and spell- 
ing books. We want nothing. The reward books, 
I doubt not, will be ready also. Though the subscrip- 
tion here to the school did not amount to four pounds 
last year, yet I ventured, and got through it without 
any thing like a burden that was grievous. To work 
hard, pay rent, and bear incidental or accidental ex- 
penses, will, I trust, always be a luxury. We have 
such a show of sweet faces, that puts me and mine in 
raptures; such a set of clean faces, so healthy, so 
happy, it does me good to look at them; and to see 
them come tumbling in, full of spirits, at the call of the 
bell, from the neighboring green lanes and fields, is 
delightful. In the course of a few years, if the Lord 
spares my life, with the help of my dear wife and 
those working under us, I shall have given religious 
ideas to a large portion of the inhabitants around us, 
This, connected with the morning congregation, the 
Tuesday evening, the Sabbath morning, and the Sab- 
bath schools and adults, whom Mrs. K. attends to on 
the Lord’s day afternoon, may give a tone to a large 
portion of the city in afew years. I believe we are - 
both solemnly dedicated to God in this work. We have 
lost some of our best girls, who could not raise the 
two-pence per week. Necessity set the brains to work, 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 57 


and produced a set of cards. I send you one for in- 
spection. But then it will destroy the subscriptions, 
people say. But when we cannot depend upon four 
pounds per annum, who is to care? I do not send the 
card to excite your liberality : you want no excitement. 
On Whit-Monday we invited all the morning children 
to breakfast on hot rolls and coffee: one hundred and 
forty-two were present. In the afternoon we hada 
grand gala of the school of industry. This was a high 
treat, and the Sabbath afternoon school was admitted 
to the festival—governesses, master, and assistants, all, 
all met; one hundred and twenty-nine, to tea!” 


In the year 1822, the chapel in South-street, where 
Mr. Kilpin preached, most unexpectedly threatened a 
speedy fall. The foundation had given way. Some 
smugglers, who lived in the vicinity, had undermined 
it many years before, when forming a cave in connec- 
tion with their own houses, to secrete the articles 
of their unlawful traffic. Experienced builders were 
employed as inspectors, and gave a unanimous opinion 
that it was dangerous, and most probably would fall 
suddenly. On one Sabbath-day Mr. K. preached in it 
three times, and on the Wednesday following it was 
closed for ever as a place of worship. 

This was so unlooked for an event, that no previous 
preparation could have been settled to raise funds for 
rebuilding. After much deliberation, an agreement was 
made for a new chapel; Mr. K. laid the corner-stone. 
All the people subscribed as their various abilities al- 
lowed. He committed the church and congregation 
to the great Head of the church; then with anguish he 
" Jeft home and its duties to the charge of his wife, and 
commenced his collecting journey in March 1823. 


a8 LIFE OF THE 


From the first letter he wrote to her, his feelings may 
be ascertained. 


“ Here I am driven from my beloved home, solitary 
and alone, a wanderer, with a life of vagrancy for a 
season in view. He who sees all things can alone de- 
scribe my conflicts. I cannot conceive that my poor 
nerves can remain unbroken a fortnight. This I must 
refer to Him, I can scarcely venture to think on home, 
sweet home. I expect to die a martyr to this dreadful 
business; but I dare not resign my office while my 
flock are in such cireumstances—it would be cowardly 
to retreat at such a period.” 

The receipt of this letter determined his wife imme- 
diately to join him; the arrangement was made, and 
in twenty-four hours after he met her at the London 
coach-office. He again became somewhat domestic; 
was in comfortable lodgings, and had the assistance he 
so much needed in preparing circulars, &c. 

Every thing that kindness could devise was done 
by many friends to lessen his trouble and expedite the 
painful business, yet every day increased its burden. 
The return of the Sabbath brought his pastoral feel- 
ings into awful exercise. He would exclaim, “ Alas, 
alas! my scattered flock are exposed to the lions and 
bears of the forest, without a shepherd to warn them 
of their danger! And perhaps the little foxes will spoil 
the choice grapes in the dear enclosure.” Then tears 
and prayers would intermingle. 

While staying in London Mr. Kilpin generally 
preached three or four times each week; he was also 
enabled to attend the anniversaries of religious insti- 
tutions, held during the month of May, 1823. These 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 59 


he enjoyed’ fnuch, and spoke of them as real cordials 
under the fatigues and trials attendant upon his expe- 
dition. In several of these meetings he took a part, 
and many who were present will long recollect the 
simple energy with which he spoke. At the meeting 
of an institution connected with Ireland, his address 
was the means of raising extraordinary contributions 
to the amount of £590. With the union of all parties 
at the meeting of the Bible Society he was much de- 
lighted. At the meeting of the Religious Tract Socie- 
ty he spoke with a feeling which showed how dear 
that institution was to him, and the simplicity with 
which he explained the varied methods by which he 
had for years circulated the publications of the society, 
excited much interest, while the numerous instances of 
usefulness to souls which he detailed, caused many of 
his auditors to lift up their hearts with gratitude to the 
Most High, while they were affected to tears by the 
circumstances he narrated. 

He was also deeply interested by several matters of 
aless public nature. One of these, as the individual 
referred to has since entered into her rest, may be no- 
ticed here. He says, in a letter to a friend, “ Yester- 
day I had an interesting day at I preached to 
a congregation of fifty lads, from fourteen to twenty- 
two years of age. They are rescued from the jaws of 
destruction, and many had the appearance of such as 
would have bid fair to be hanged! They are now 
taught to read. They sung sweetly—some of them 
were obliged to weep, a thing they, perhaps, never did 
before! It seemed as if they were frightened at their 
own feelings. They afterward came round me, thrust- 
~ yng their hands over the shoulders of each other to 
shake hands with me. The good iady only hinted 


60 LIFE OF THE 


whether they would be glad to see me again, and in- 
stantly they threw up their hands. They promised 
to pray over the text, Jer. 3:4, ‘Wilt thou not from 
this time cry unto me, My Father, thou art the guide 
of my youth?” 

During this visit to the metropolis Mr. Kilpin did not 
neglect his favorite occupation of distributing tracts. 
One extract must suffice. “I gave to the Sunday 
newspaper man something else to read, and distributed 
fifty of the tract On Eternity to groups of sinners, ag 
they were pouring in throngs from London to Green- 
wich. To me it was anew sight, and awful beyond 
description. I involuntarily said, O London, are these 
thy inhabitants !” 

But he was not insensible to the more pleasing fea~ 
tures of the great city. In another letter he says, 
“London tried me many ways; but it has more im- 
pressed my mind with the true religion and real bene- 
volence to be found there than I had any idea of be- 
fore. <A visit, a preaching visit—not a begging one, 
may that never again be my lot—a preaching visit to 
London in another year, would be hailed as a luxury. 
London has endeared itself to me on many accounts.” 

His stay in London was hurried to a close by a cir- 
cumstance which tried his feelings. For several weeks 
he had not met with a single instance of unkindness 
from any one upon whom he called. His God gave 
him favor with members of various denominations, 
and his collections were unusually large. He remitted 
the amount with regularity, and it was sufficient to 
meet every demand while the work was in progress, 
so that till the whole was completed no one was called 
"upon to advance a shilling. But one day he returned 
earlier than usual and entered his bed-room, as was 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 61 


his daily custom, before the domestic refreshment was 
tasted, to pour out his overburdened soul to Him who 
could sustain it. As he remained longer than common, 
his wife entered and found him in bed, in such a situ- 
ation of body and mind as made it necessary immedi- 
ately to leave London. Ina few hours he was in the 
coach on the road to Newport Pagnel, his beloved sis- 
ter’s residence, which he reached on the 24th of July. 
He had met with some unfeeling, unkind men, who 
had opened the wounds of mental sorrow and caused 
them to bleed at every pore. 

After a few weeks spent among his friends and rela- 
tives at Bedford and the vicinity, he again opened his 
painful commission, but with increased bitterness of 
feeling. On entering one city the gaol met his eye, 
when he and his wife agreed that if they possessed 
the liberty of choice, they would most cheerfully have 
resided within its walls (any breach of the laws of 
God and man to justify such confinement being out of 
the question) for cne month, rather than spend that 
period on a begging excursion, although, as was the 
case, every thing that friendship and kindness could 
administer to alleviate, awaited them in an unusual 
degree. Succéss, as to the object, he continued to ob- 
tain, until he reached his beloved home, after an ab- 
sence of six months, which was the most painful and 
injurious period both to body and soul in his history. 

The contract for the building was £1800; more than 
£1300 was collected. He gave the most minute and 
particular report of his collections, &c., which was au- 
dited by his deacons; and he had the unspeakable 
pleasure of paying from his own resources every per- 
sonal expense during the tour.

Shortly after, he took a general retrospect of this 
journey in a letter to a friend. He says: 


“T have reviewed my journey and peregrinations, 
and am amazed at the scene, and filled with admiration 
at the goodness of our God. Had I known its difficul- 
ties beforehand, my soul had fainted within me; but 
the God whom we served, helped daily. I had all I 
asked for in every place.—My Sabbaths every where, 
generally speaking, were high manifestations of the 
love of God. Many times have I been in such ecsta- 
cies, while feeling his glorious presence in my soul, 
that a little more, and the desire, the ardent desire, the 
expectation, the often anticipated expectation, of dying 
in the pulpit, would have been granted. 

“T hope I weigh all things in the balances of eternity, 
and though I have as many mercies (circumscribed, 
yet the circle is large enough for me and mine) as any 
one, they have no charms but as they lead me to God, 
or help me to serve God. I have no wish for one shil- 
ling undedicated to God. During my late journey I 
have seen the rich and the poor; I have seen wealth 
and grandeur, want and poverty; and I see that they 
are of equal account in His hands who weighs and 
measures all things. He maketh the rich; he maketh 
the poor. The happiness and satisfaction which they 
severally feel or want, are just in proportion as they 
seek their happiness in God, or endeavor, in vain, to 
find itin earthly things. The man that is surrounded 
with all which this world can bestow, may enjoy God 
in all; and he that has comparatively nothing, may 
enjoy allin God. It is not so much the circumstances 
‘we are in, as the spirit we are in under them. How 
many times did that sentiment occur to me while walk-

ing, weeping, and praying, in the streets of your city!”

O blessed Redeemer, O my Lord and Savior, thou didst

pay for us!

chief.”

“Tam certainly better. The people say, ‘He will 
do yet! The affection of the poor is very affecting. 
If we stand in the street to speak to any one, a little 
hand will creep into ours; a touch on the skirt will 
indicate some little soul is glad; some will stop and 
be kissed before they wiil let us go. These little ones 
are our charms. I have met and spoken to thousands 
of children since I left London.” 


In one of the preceding letters Mr. Kilpin mentions 
that he had undertaken to superintend a depository for 
the sale of the publications of the Religious Tract So- 
ciety. A few particulars relative to his efforts for pro- 
moting that cause may be here mentioned. Mr. Kilpin 
had been for many years an extensive distributer of 
tracts. Though not in direct correspondence with the 
Parent Society, he had seen numerous instances of use- 
fulness resulting from the operations of the Institution. 
In the autumn of 1819 he undertook to devote the 
room, intended as a study for his deceased son, to the 
storing and sale of its publications. ‘This labor of love 
he undertook to superintend without any remunera- 
tion, and to collect subscriptions, and to supply sub- 
scribers in the same manner as at the depository of 
the parent Society, being merely repaid the expense 
of carriage and other incidental charges. The ar- 
rangement was very important, as there was not any 
auxiliary for the Tract Society in Exeter, or the imme- 
diate vicinity, at that time in existence. 

Mr. Kilpin conducted this work with his accustomed 
zeal and indefatigable attention, and continued it till a 
few months before his death. At one time the sales 
were considerable; the labor to Mr. Kilpin was very 
great, and trying in many respects. He availed him 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN 65 


self of every opportunity to make the society known, 
and succeeded in establishing several auxiliaries; he 
also visited several parts of England, at different times 
to plead in behalf of the Institution. At home, his 
efforts for the circulation of tracts were unremitting: 
one espevially requires distinct notice. 

In 1821, the committee were anxious that a large 
quantity of hroad-sheets should be placed on the walls 
of cottages, factories, &c. with the view of excluding 
the pernicious trash usually affixed on them. In no 
part of England was this more desirable than in the 
west, and Mr. Kilpin, with his accustomed energy, 
offered to make arrangements for the due circulation 
of twenty thousand sheets. He found many who were 
willing to engage in this work, but determined to part 
with none of the broad-sheets without a written en- 
gagement from the party receiving them, that they 
would stick up, or see stuck up, the whole number 
allotted to them, and endeavor to displace the songs, 
ribaldry, infidel publications, and trash, already upon 
the walls. By this means efficient co-operation was 
obtained, while much moral contamination was pre- 
vented by the removal of the pernicious publications 
which deformed the interior of the buildings entered 
by these active agents. Much good resulted from the 
divine blessing on the contents of the papers by which 
the walls were now adorned. In the Reports of the 
Tract Society for 1822 and 1823, the reader will find 
some interesting particulars relative to this work, and 
there is the fullest reason to believe that these broad- 
sheets were instrumental in the conversion of several 
persons. 

This attack upon a principal means of advancing 
evil, produced some animadyversicns in one of the infi- 


66 LIFE OF THE 


del publications of the day, which represented it as a 
crusade of gloomy religionists, &c. against the “old 
English ballads,” and an attempt to change “the popu-

fanaticism! Upon the malignant absurdity of such 
allegations it is unnecessary to remark; but they in- 
duced some of the commiitee to examine a large quan- 
tity of the “stuff” which had been removed from the 
walls, with the consent of the owners, to substitute the 
publications of the Society, and which Mr. Kilpin had 
forwarded to London as an evidence of the beneficial 
results of the labors of those who exerted themselves 
for this work.* In this mass of corruption, the relic 
of popery, called “Our Savior’s Letter,” was promi- 
nent; upwards of thirty distinct editions of it were 
ascertained, and this mischievous sheet was perhaps. 
the most innocent of the whole. Not a single speci- 
men of the simple ballads of our ancestors was found , 
a proof of, the change produced by the infidel and de- 
moralizing principles which of late years have beer. 
actively disseminated. Several persons in other parts 
of England, hearing of Mr. Kilpin’s labors in this 
branch of tract operations, were induced to go and do 
likewise. “The Savior’s Letter” was found univer-

Mr. Kilpin also endeavored to promote the sale of 
other works of general utility, but various causes pre- 
vented him from carrying this part of his design to 
any extent; his efforts were almost exclusively con- 
fined to the publications of the Tract Society: in at- 

_tending to them he found full occupation for the time 
he could devote to this object; and the consideration 
that it was, as he expressed, “an important link in a 
chain” for doing good, alone supported him under 
much that he experienced in this work. His personal 
aid, as already stated, was afforded gratuitously, but he 
was often annoyed by the conduct of some among his 
poorer customers, who would tell him that they had 
no doubt he made money by it! while others, whose 
means were ample, and who ought to have known bet- 
ter, treated him as if he lived by their custom, instead 
of remembering that they were obliged to him for en- 
abling them to procure supplies of desirable publica- 
tions formerly inaccessible. The remembrance of these 
annoya.ces, however, soon passed away, and the re- 
collection subsequently afforded amusement, while the 
opportunity he had of explaining the manner in which 
Tract distribution ought to be conducted, and of intro- 
ducing the real doctrines of the Gospel, abundantly 


68 MIrE OF THE 4 


repaid the petty vexations which occurred. He thus 
wrote to a friend: 


“Two ladies came to-day; they looked at Tracts 
selected some—tour pages—one of each, rejected 
others, &c. &c., and so on for a full hour on Saturday 
morning, and after all the lot came to sixpence! But 
what think you? One of them produced twelve Tracts 
to be changed. She had come fourteen miies. ‘O 
Madam,’ I exclaimed, ‘ I would have given them to the 
wind, and two hundred more with them, in the dis- 
tance you came” I prevailed, and she took them back. 
The thought that her nine-penny worth might perhaps 
be the means of converting nine souls, calmed all, and 
most devoutly do I pray the Lord to bless her, and en- 
large her ideas.” 


At another time, he wrote: 


“T will tell you one depot anecdote which excels all 
preceding ones. A lady sent for a copy of the Tract 
‘To the Afilicted,’ saying, ‘ Please to send one, and I 
will send the money.’ She sent twenty-five miles for 
it, but I was only to send one. It is a penny Tract; if 
approved, she would keep it, and send the money— 
one penny: She is worth many, many thousands of 
pounds.” But all Mr. Kilpin’s Tract customers were 
not of this description ; several might be mentioned, 
who devised liberal things, and by his efforts as a Tract 
depositary, were induced to come forward and assist — 
the work of God in various other ways to a consider- 
able extent. In another letter he says, ‘It needs the 
patience of Job to serve hawkers, and the eye of a 
hawk to see that they do not cheat.’ ‘Please, sir, to 
let me have four-pennyworth of all sorts—now please 


REY. SAMUEL KILPIN. 68 


to give one in, for Iam a poor man,’ &c. They come 
drunk, and plead poverty; of course they have a lec- 
ture into the bargain.” 


The Religious Tract Society was an object always 
dear te him; he especially delighted in it, as the good 
resulting from its operations were so evidently the 
work of God, not the work of man. He ever viewed 
it as an institution most especially blessed of God in 
the conversion of souls. 

Mr. Kilpin rejoiced in witnessing the recent exten- 
sion of the Society, and received much gratification 
and benefit from-perusing many of their volumes. The 
‘Daily Food’ was a constant companion during a great 
part of his illness, as it has been to many others in 
similar situations; his diary showed the support he 
often derived from the portions of Scripture. 

One who was best acquainted with his views, and 
continually witnessed his labors, says: 


“The Religious Tract Society was a subject to which 
he was always alive. I suppose he never entered a 
party where the object was not known, without intro- 
ducing it in some way; nor did he ever reside a week 
in any place without endeavoring to form a society, or 
to rouse every friend whom he could influence, to ex- 
ertions in its behalf. It is unnecessary to say that he 
rejoiced at its progress and success. When in his de- 
pository at work, a man of the world would have ima- 
gined that, instead of being a gratuitous laborer, his 
maintenance depended on the sale of publications. He 
sacrificed time and personal and domestic comforts, to 
avery great extent, to promote this work. He was 

always speculating how to promote the interests of the 


"0 LIFE OF THE 


Society ; and his views were not confined to specula- 
tion, they were carried into action to the utmost of his 
power. 

“ Asa distributer, his labors were incessant; he al- 
lowed himself ten thousand a year for personal distri- 
bution. His anxiety made him very expert in catching 
opportunities. A volume might be written of the suc- 
cess with which it pleased God to bless his efforts. In 
short, Tracts were thoroughly blended with the duties, 
the business, and employments of Mr. Kilpin’s life for 
many years. 

“ He circulated large quantities of Tracts by sup- 
plying persons disposed to itinerate and sell them: I 
will mention only one instance. A man, ragged and 
dirty, but whose manners indicated something different 
from his outward appearance, asked for a few Tracts. 
Some were given. He sold them, and brought part of 
the money to purchase more. This was repeated for 
several days. Mr. Kilpin then told him that his charac- 
ter certainly was an assumed one; that he had seen 
better times; that he felt interested for him, and, if he 
would be frank, he would endeavor to assist him. The 
poor fellow was taken by surprise, and disclosed his 
history. Mr. Kilpin then set him up as a traveling 
Tract man. He succeeded so well, that in a few months 
he returned well dressed, with the appearance and 
manners of a gentleman, so that he was not recognized 
till he announced himself as the Tract traveler. He 
was the son of a respectable merchant in America, who 
was reduced, by his own imprudence, to the miserable 
plight in which he first appeared. Mr. Kilpin con- 
versed and prayed with him: both wept. He obtained 
a passage to America, and returned to his friends, who 
were ignorant of his situation.” 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. V4 


Several such persons were usually going through 
arious parts of the country; once as many as eight at 
the same time. Some of these occasional venders did 
not turn out so well as in the preceding instance, but 
there was always the consolation that the Tracts were 
spread abroad, and might prove beneficial in places in- 
accessible to other means of instruction; and the out- 
lay of a few Tracts, and occasionally a little temporal 
relief, was no subject for regret, even if the distributer 
did not prove worthy. Persons residing in the coun- 
try have many opportunities of this nature, and per- 
haps the mention of them may induce some to avail 
themselves of such as occur. The writer proceeds: 


“Mr. Kilpin’s heart and bodily strength were so 
completely incorporated in the Tract cause, that when 
he discontinued his depository, he felt as though de- 
prived of alimb. If it had pleased God to restore his 
health, he intended to recommence his labors in that 
work. He reckoned the work an honor, though often 
blamed, and even ridiculed, by those who ought to 
have dasisted him. To mix with what they termed 
vulgar characters, and “to be publicly seen mixed up 
with such trifles,” was deemed by them to be low and 
degrading to the character of the ministry ; but the re- 
sult has shown the contrary, and that, had he abstained 
from the use of such means, he would have relinquished 
the most effectual weapon in his power for attacking 
the strong holds of Satan, and, as far as he was con- 
cerned, many souls might have perished who now are 
rejoicing in the well-founded hope, or in actual pos- 
session of eternal happiness.” 


Mr Kilpin met with numerous instances of useful- 


72 LIFE OF THE 


ness to parents from lending Tracts to the children 
who attended his morning prayers. One he thus re- 
lated : 


“The mother was an industrious respectable woman, 
but quite ignorant as to the way of salvation; the fa- 
ther, one who had been ensnared by drunkenness, and 
seldom came home sober. They had three children; 
the eldest nine years old. ‘The children attended the 
little chapel at early prayers, and received the Tracts 
for several weeks; they were read at home, and the 
parents thought Mr. Kilpin must be aware of all the 
particulars of their lives, but wondered how he ob- 
tained the information. At length the mother asked 
the boy, ‘John, how is it Mr. Kilpin picks them so for 
you? ‘Why, mother, he gives them us as fast as he 
can; he never picks them for any body.’ This made 
it worse. In fact, I did not know either of them. One 
day the child said, ‘ Mother, do come up on Tuesday 
night, and hear Mr. Kilpin.’ Others had tried to per- 
suade her, but in vain; the child, however, succeeded. 
She said, ‘I liked the minister, and he shook hands 
with me! so she came again. The second time, the 
Lord sent home the word to her, and she began to 
pray. ‘This is now twelve months since, and she is a 
member of our church, lively and anxious to bring all 
to God. Her husband was struck with the Tracts; al- 
most angry that they were sent, yet could not help 
reading them. It was observed that the eldest boy, 
instead of getting into bed, and saying his prayers 
there, would say them out of bed. (I had said, speak- 
ing to the children, on a text in Canticles, ‘I sought 
him on my bed, but found him not.’ ‘Sought him on 
_ my bed! only a lazy Christian will do so, if one at all. 
Ah, my dear children, that is the way many of you do, 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 3 


and so fall asieep,’&c.) After getting into bed, he was 
observed to be very still. This was noticed by the pa- 
rents, who inquired the reason. The little brother said, 
* John lies and prays for father” ‘What does he say ? 
John was called to explain. ‘I don’t know what to 
say, but Mr. Kilpin says we must pray for our father ; 
and so I say,—O Lord, do not let father get drunk!’ 
This roused the father’s feelings; he was touched ; he 
inquired for himself; and now he prays for himself 
and for them. He is regular in attendance on public 
worship, and has not been intoxicated for nine months, 
but comes home in the evening and reads to his wife. 
*O, sir, said she, ‘now we know what happiness is!’ 
Here then there is hope that a family has been rescued. 
The children are brought up with regularity, and 
some of their connections come to hear the word also. 
Neither the Religious Tract Society’s Committee nor 
myself have a right to any of the glory, but we have 
a share in the pleasure. The glory be only to our 
God.” 


Another method of interesting children, which Mr. 
Kilpin practised for some time, was allowing those 
who attended him in a morning, to write any text they 
pleased on a slip of paper; he would then collect them 
in a bundle, and drawing some of them out without se- 
lection, would make some apposite remarks. The texts 
selected were sometimes not exactly such as Mr. Kilpin 
would have preferred, but he always found something 
to say, and seldom without leaving animpression. The 
text above mentioned probably was one of these.

“ February 5, 1822. 

“Dear sir,—Our friends say, ‘Try, and if it suc- 
ceeds, we'will help you.’ This is like committing a 
vessel, that could sail if she were manned, to the rocks, 
without hands. Here is a vessel, but no hands; here 
is a heart, but no power. I owe no man any thing, and 
I have no personal wants but what can be proportioned 
to my income, therefore I must beg the £10 may be 
the first contribution to the boys’ school. I have put it 
in the savings’ bank, with two pounds given by another 
friend. Ihave my awful fears about the boys’ school. 
I am willing to give four guineas per annum, and su- 
perintend gratis; but the fit-up, and the master’s salary 
alarm me. If ever two persons on earth did agree to 
ask one thing of God, and yet submit to the divine di- 
rection—nay, to wish the Lord to shut up the way, ix 
it was not for his glory, and not merely for the benefit 
of the children and their parents, and if his honor is 
not especially concerned, we are thus agreed not to 
wish it. This would add to my labor; but I would ra- 
ther have a mountain resting on my frame, than sit on 
a hill uselessly enjoying the beautiful prospect. We 
are now as busy as bees in summer, at our cot of con- 
tentment. I forgot to mention we have established a 
juvenile library, and I have enlarged my stock of sell- 
ing books. I think this will prove to be of God. You 
will be delighted with the living mass of souls con- 
nected with this little missionary station. When I think 
that the Lord hath enabled me to wade through waters 
more than ancle deep, with dark clouds over my head, 
my feelings are overcome. I bless God and say, Though 
now in much care, no one on earth has more reason to 
_ praise God for spiritual and temporal mercies, with the 
hopes of eternal happiness, than we have. My dear 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 75 


wife and myself in heart are one. We work, and pray, 
and sit, and weep, and sing together. She says, ‘If we 
doubted as to the future, it would be a sin of the most 
aggravated nature; to which I add, Amen. 

“ Our prayers are daily waiting, our faith is strug- 
gling to establish a refuge for orphans, and an acade- 
my to educate the sons and daughters of poor dissent- 
ing ministers, and to educate a few village preachers. 
My friends often consider these things as eccentric and 
enthusiastic ; but, hitherto, my God has said it is not 
enthusiasm. I mean still to trust, and goon. He, whom 
E serve, knows when it is needful, and always sends 
help after faith, or to encourage faith. My ardent soul 
aspires to go on without foreign aid; but I must con- 
tinue to live by faith until that time arrive. I have now 
an inflammation on my chest, from the last easterly 
winds. We meet with much ingratitude and wicked - 
ness, but with more real distress and gratitude. There 
are some forged bank-notes, but who will throw away 
the good ones, because there are some forgeries? nor 
must we neglect deserving objects, because there are 
vile impostors. 

“Thank you for the latter part of your letter, where 
you say ‘ you will remember me to your heavenly Fa- 
ther” He knows me well; whatever is good, has been 
derived from him, as the sole Author; and his Son has 
promised to answer for all that is bad; and his Holy 
Spirit has promised to prepare me by his holy unction. 
Thus, through the blessed Trinity, one God, I shall 
have the happiness of meeting you in heaven.” 


To another Friend.

place in St. Thomas’s: there are nearly four thousand

one small church. I have been waiting for some weeks 
and months to go out on the Irish school expedition; 
the way hitherto has been hedged up. 

“You inquire if I have met with any fresh instance 
of conversion amongst children. I went into my gar- 
den of nuts and pomegranates, of sweet little clusters, 
to see if any fresh seed had been sown and was spring- 
ing up. I went to the ehamber of a sick boy, eleven 
years old; he was very ill. I asked him which he 
would rather do, live or die? ‘Oh,’ said he, ‘I had 
rather go with death—I am not afraid of death—I 
hope I should go to heaven: was I to live, I fear I 
should return again to sin. I love you, sir, I love my 
mother, but I love Christ better: he died for sinners, 
and he said, Come unto me, all ye that aber.’ Then 
you have no fear of death? I said. ‘No,’ said he, ‘I 
pray to Christ.’ 

“ Nothing can tempt this child from religion—from 
prayer. What are all the pleasures of life compared to 
the charms in this poor garret, this poor helpless child, 
my spiritual child, leaning on the bosom of his tender 
Shepherd? I feel honored by leading the soul of this 
little one to the Friend of children, and none shall 
pluck him out of thy hand, dear Savior! What will 
the resurrection-day exhibit, when God comes to 
reckon his triumphs of grace? There will then be a 
goodly number of these little ones appear to have been 
born here; these are greater triumphs than all the

at the footstool of our Father, than any victorious ge-

to feed the body and to clothe it, that is all I want. 
Another of the Savior’s babes is gone to glory ; he died

Last evening I took the hand of a man between eighty

with trembling lips, and with a feeling in which his 
own soul was interested ; ‘and have to bless God, sir,

year ago, this man knew no more of God than the dis-

and ventures on the Lord Jesus Christ as his only Sa- 
vior. No sword will cut, except it be used, nor ham- 
mer break, except the blow is given; but it is a cheer- 
ing fact, that no one can use either spiritual hammer 
or sword with simplicity of heart, but God the Holy 
Ghost will bless the attempt. We go on well, sail to- 
lerably fast—the vessel is deeply laden, but sails the 
more steady. The church in South-street was never 
better, never poorer, never richer.”

I had made a matter of prayer, and repeatedly asked

“We have divided the school while the building is 
going on. One half goes to the parlor, the other are 
under my study. I was fearful the children would in- 
terrupt me, but they do not, they only excite my 
prayers to God for them. 

“Tam loaded with mercies, yet have much to exer- 
cise flesh and blood, grace and graces. I much need 
your prayers. I never love money in any other way 
but as the farmer does manure, to enrich the soil, and 
produce a crop. Riches are no sign of covenant love; 
God gives them to his worst enemies. To have the 
power of giving is delightful; to receive is blessed, 
but to give is more blessed—it is more like the great 
_ Benefactor—it shows the heart of the giver more than 
the receiver; it shows that the Lord has a high opinion 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 79 


of his heart, of his head, of his hands: it is a sign of 
victory over the world; it is an enviable station in hu- 
man life. A gentleman once said to me, he was glad 
that I did not live near him; I should tempt him to 
empty his pockets as I did my own: if I had a thou- 
sand a-year, I would spend it. May the Lord enable 
me to work with the tools I have, and use the means 
which he has put into my hands. I have devoted my- 
self solemnly to God, and have not one single wish to 
live but for his glory and the welfare of others. I am 
sure there is not a village within ten miles around us 
but would be glad to have preaching and Sunday 
schools. [am rather poorly to-day from over exercise. 
It is true that things may be worn out, but it is more 
true that rust will spoil them faster: better wear out 
than rust out. Soon we must all move off; blessed be 
God, there remaineth a rest for the people of God; 
but I hope there will be something to do in that world 
also.” 


These schools engaged much of the energies of Mr. 
Kilpin and his beloved partner. Their souls delighted 
in the work, but they experienced many painful trials 
in conducting them. The inward contests which en- 
sued, and the manner in which they were overcome, 
are graphically depictured in the following letter: 


“My pear rrienp,—I will give you my morning 
temptation and soliloquy. Sometimes I converse aloud 
with myself. 

* A combination of dead, or rather living flies, from 
every department in the schools and their connections, 
gained the victory over my temper. I opened my ac- 
~ count-book, took my pen, and was about to insert, 


80 LIFE OF THE 


‘Settled in full” Reasoning angrily, I began: ‘ Why 
should I be teazed in this way ? what are the children 
and their parents to me? Here I am, tugging and 
toiling, writing and exhorting, until 1am quite weary. 
Added to this, lam contracting personal obligations 
for them; for some of our good friends will not see 
the difference between giving to me personally, and 
assisting me as an agent in the cause of God.’ 

“T have often to contend with pecuniary difficulties, 
because I am always obliged to advance a part from 
my little income to meet the weekly demands for the 
schools, and, as an individual, Ihave to answer for eve- 
ry shilling. Meanwhile my ignorant neighbors are 
saying—‘ Ah! it is no wonder he is going on so well, 
when he gains so much from the school and tracts? 
One man had just told me, in the tract depository, that 
he dared to say I gained a good fat living amongst it 
all! Here old Adam swelled, and reared his head ma- 
jestically high—lI felt mortified, not humbled. But to 
return to my self-conversation, I could not but ask,— 
‘Did you not begin the schools with prayer? Yes. 
Have they not been continued with prayer? Yes. 
Has not the blessing of God been sought from day to 
day? Yes. Does God owe you any thing? No. 
Then do you intend to close them without prayer? I 
cannot pray about them. Then wait until you can! 
Well, that is reasonable. Take your Bible instead of 
your account-book, and hear what advice that will 
give. Well, so I will. I opened that blessed volume

himself, lest ye be weary and faint in your minds, 
This is rather an embarrassing text; let me inquire 
about it. Whois He? The King of kings and Lord

of lords. For what purposes did he endure such con-

Mr. Kilpin’s study, and the room used as a tract de- 
pository, were adjoining, and detached from the house. 
In these much of ministerial labor was combined. 
Here he had many interviews with inquiring souls; 
here he pointed them to the Savior, and knelt with 


82 LIFE OF THE 


them in prayer, often pleading for them with tears. 
Persons of various countries, characters, and denomi- 
nations, were his visitors, wd shared his prayers and 
affectionate solicitude. When the great and blessed 
God makes up his jewels, it will, there can be no doubt, 
be said of many, They were born there. These rooms 
were sanctified by the word of God and prayer. Here 
he kept days of fasting and intercession. He often 
prostrated himself on the floor, and with strong cries 
and groans wept over the sins and personal guilt of 
men, and agonized for the increase of the Savior’s 
kingdom. Here he hada list of names, personal friends 
and their families, whom he presented daily to his God- 
Often a new name was added, but none were erased 
until they had left this world for abetter. A real friend- 
ship once formed with Mr. Kilpin was not likely to be 
dissolved on either side. 

For some years he dispensed medicine to the poor. 
Many patients daily attended. This was a valuable 
opportunity of addressing their souls. His study-door 
was open from ten o’clock until one, to admit all who 
came on business relative to their souls or bodies. His 
attention to the bodily wants of the poor was unremit- 
ting. He knew that kindness of this description was 
often made the means of benefiting the soul. He thus 
introduced one of his plans to a friend: 


“Winter is coming, and many persons with us will 
be half-starved. Iam offered ten pounds’ worth of good 
new blankets very cheap, and want to purchase them 
for lending to the poor; one or two blankets, just as 
the necessity of the case may be, from'December to 
March, to be returned clean, sixpence heing first paid 
for the use; the blankets to be marked so that they ean: 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 83 


not be sold. It would make me happy, when warm, 
thus to help some child of God. It would also extend 
our influence and bring them to the house of God; and 
if they should be brought to seek Christ’s garment of 
righteousness to clothe, defend, ornament, and justify, 
how exhilarating the result!’ Now will you guarantee 
me in saying, ‘I want to raise a little money to pur- 
chase blankets to lend to the poor for the winter?’ A 
friend of mine says, that if I can raise £5 in a given 
time, he will answer for £5 more, and I have the offer 
of some, &c. Will youhelp this plan? He that 
clothes the lilies of the valley could as easily clothe 
the poor; but he commits them to our care. He who 
says, ‘If thine enemy hunger, feed him,’ says also, 
*Thou shalt surely help thy poor brother,’ &c. 

“Now it is natural for you to say, ‘The poor we have 
always with us, and Exeter must help Exeter.’ This 
is true, and Exeter has helped Exeter. By various 
aids I have helped poor creatures to the amount of £50 
last year; but I want to gain still more influence over 
the poor, to bring them to our dear Lord. I felt as 
though I should be consumed, when I thought of Him 
that was rich, but for our sakes became poor, when last 
year I saw poor people of God, princes in disguise, 
whose shoes I was not worthy to unloose, wasting away 
in affliction, and could not cover them. I began this 
plan last year, but failed through a sudden gust of wind! 
now I am willing to try again. 

“Tf you do not see this as I do, let it pass unnoticed. 
The poor here have no legitimate claims on you, nor 
do I pretend to say it is your duty to do this. The 
fact is, simply, that I am surrounded with the poor, 
and have no expenses I ean lessen to help them. 
Their cries pierce my heart; they hang upon me as a 


64 LIFD OF THE 


sort of medical, spiritual father, and nothing but good- 
ness of heart in some of my friends could bear with 
my solicitations.” * 


From a subsoquent letter it appears that the plan for 
blankets sueceeded. The expense of drugs alone dis- 
pensed by Mr. Kilpin sometimes exceeded £10 in the 
year. He was, in fact, always “devising liberal things,” 
and when his own limited means failed, further assist- 
ance generally came, though seldom, if ever, to an ex- 
tent equal to the expanded desires of his heart. Under 
the pressure of such feelings he wrote: 


“No two people on earth can need you~ prayers 
more than Mrs. K. and myself. Men of large personal 
property may attempt things, and get through them, 
without simply looking to God, and, if they fail in 
their object, they have only to say, ‘Never mind; but 
all we attempt we are forced to bore through, and 
many a night do I lie awake with cold sweats occa- 
sioned by unbelieving prudentia’ fears. Sometimes I 
rise quite sick with the reproaches of the enemy,— 
‘Where now is your God? You! what you dosuch a 
thing as this? You will bein jail with all your whims 
and enthusiasm, and then your friends will be the first 
to reproach and the-last to help you. You are nar- 
rowly watched, and many eyes are on you, waiting for 
your halting. Lay aside such exertions; but few will 
thank you for them. Why cannot you go through the 
world calmly? Give up/these fancies and lead a quiet 
life’ These reproaches of the adversary have been 
repelled by sweet portions of Scripture, or lines of 
hymns, particularly : 


REY. SAMUEL KILPIN. 85 


‘Make you his service your delight, 
‘He’ll make your wants his care.’ 


*Lackest thou any thing? is another repeller to his 
face. And when retiring for morning secret devo- 
tion, such an out-poured overwhelming sense of the 
divine presence, and frequently such an appearance of 
Providence is vouchsafed, that victory is proclaimed. 
But these suggestions of the enemy are not all lies. 
The father of lies and the children of lies always tell 
some truths mixed with the lies, and sometimes they 
are interwoven with such subtlety, that, till we strict- 
ly examine their tendency, it is not easy to discern 
which are the falsehoods; but that always detects 
them.” 


The following memorandum has reference to Mr. 
Kulpin’s labors as a two-fold physician, and shows the 
awe with which even the profane were impressed by 
his affectionate, yet solemn admonitions: “December 
20th, 1823, preached a funeral-sermon from Ecclesias- 
tes, 11:3, ‘In the place where the tree falleth, there it 
shall be.’ The subject was an infidel: he came to me 
for medicine. I made him some pills, with a charge 
that he should pray for a blessing on them. He would 
not; and after what had been said to him, he dared 
not take them without prayer. His cough came on; 
a blood-vessel burst;—death followed.” 


Another object which engaged Mr. Kilpin’s warmest 
sympathies, and some of his most active energies, was 
the state of those unhappy females who abound in our 
large cities: he was instrumental in rescuing several 


from their wretched life. In this work Mrs. Kilpin 
8 Kilpin. 


86 LIFE OF THE 


_ also was ever ready and enterprising. Several deeply 
interesting instances of usefulness in this way oc- 
curred, but the particulars must be withheld. Letters 
upon this subject cannot be submitted to the public 
eye, or details might here be given of the most affect- 
ing nature. The painful cireumstances which thus 
continually came to their knowledge, stimulated them 
to increasing activity in opposing the kingdom of dark- 
ness, and the horrid depravity of the unconverted heart 
was more clearly exhibited to them. Here again they 
marked, that ignorance was the fertile source of vice, 
and thus they were encouraged in their efforts for im- 
parting instruction to the young. 

Mr. Kilpin very rarely paid any visit of a formal de- 
scription. It was matter of regret to him that so little 
desire for pastoral visits was manifested. He remarked, 
“Do parents notice the diseases of their children’s bo- 
dies in the light manner they, for the most part, regard 
the state of their souls? Are no inquiries made rela- 
tive to symptoms of health or sickness? Is the con- 
version of the soul so trifling, of such small importance, 
so imperceptible in the life, as not to catch a parent’s 
eye, or to excite parental solicitude? O, what a minis- 
ter has to witness and to feel! What advantages have 
the children of the poor!”

know its sweetness! He returned from school, was 
in apparent health, gathering strawberries at eight 
o’elock; and was a corpse, in my arms, at eleven the 
same night!” ;

Having presented the reader with numerous details 


REV. SAMOEL KILPIN. , 89 


relative 1o what may be termed Mr. Kilpin’s private 
labors, this appears a suitable place to insert some cha- 
racteristic anecdotes respecting him in the public dis- 
charge of his ministerial duties. 

When any member of his church came with details 
of real or supposed injuries received from a fellow- 
member, after listening to the reporter, Mr. Kilpin 
would inquire if they had mentioned these grievances 
to their offending brother or sister. If the reply was 
in the negative, (and usually it was so,) he would then 
- calmly order a messenger to fetch them, remarking 

that it would be ungenerous to decide, and unscriptural 
to act merely from hearing the statement of one party. 
This determination always produced alarm, and the 
request that nothing might be mentioned to the par- 
ties implicated. This plan had a peaceful influence, 
and often produced humility and self-accusation. As- 
sertions and proofs are very different grounds for the 
exercise of judgment, and are more distinct than angry 
persons imagine. 

Mr. Kilpin, as a minister, mingled with various classes 
of society ; this gave him a quick perception of charac- 
ter, and furnished perpetual and painful evidences that 
the human heart is deceitful above all things. It was 
afrequent practice for persons to seek his advice on 
subjects which were previously settled. In one in- 
stance, a good woman came with a long preface on 

‘the duty and privilege of having the opinion of a minis- 
ter on the important subject of marriage. She told her 
tale, and sought advice. Mr. Kilpin guessed how mat- 
ters stood, and unexpectedly inquired if the day for 
her marriage was not fixed for Tuesday? “O no, 
sit,” she hastily replied, “not until Thursday.” This 
gave him an opportunity of pointing out the sin of per- 

K 3% 


90 LIFE OF THE 


sons treating the great and blessed God in somewhat 
the same manner, seeking direction on a subject clear- 
ly stated in his word, with a determination to act as 
their own feelings and desires dictated, let the voice of 
God in his word or providence be what it might. 

His appeals to the conscience were very forcible; 
they often pierced as a sword, to the dividing asunder 
of the soul and spirit, and brought to light the thoughts 
and intents of the heart. In many instances, persons 
called on him to animadvert on the extreme impro- 
priety of his conduct, in bringing family and church 
concerns, as they said, so often into the pulpit, and 
giving personal reproofs in public. After hearing their 
complaints, he had usually to inform them, that he 
had never before had the least intimation of the cir- 
cumstances they mentioned, and that many of the per- 
sons implicated were entirely unknown to him. He 
never preached a personal sermon, or degraded the 
pulpit by the introduction of church or family quarrels, 
nor did he ever make personal allusions to the impro- 
prieties and sins of his flock, but requested each of his 
hearers to allow conscience to make the application: 
as the word of God was quick and powerful, they 
might be assured it would discover their sins, if they 
were anxious for self-knowledge. 

In one instance, he had lent money, which he con- 
sidered as a lost debt. On the Sabbath his text led 
him to quote the inquiry, “ How much owest thou thy 
Lord?’ &c. On the Monday the money was brought 
in much anger. The preacher had no idea that the 
person was in the meeting-house, or that any one knew 
of the circumstance. 

The Holy Ghost bore witness to the word of his 
grace in the hearts of many strangers, who frequently 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 91 


attended, not knowing whither they went. Mr. Kil- 
pin’s soul was often cheered by unexpected recitals of 
this description. Two may be related which may en- 
courage ministers to persevere in their labors, although 
no fruit should be immediately visible. 

Mr. Kilpin had witnessed the awful consequences 
produced in the church of Christ, and in families, from 
those who professed to be the disciples of Jesus form- 
ing marriages contrary to the command, “ Be not un- 
equally yoked with unbelievers,”—“ only in the Lord,” 
&c. As he never shunned to declare the whole counsel 
of God, this subject was presented to his congregation. 
The next day, a gentleman, whose name or residence 
he never knew, called to thank him for the discourse, 
adding, that his state of mind when he entered Exeter 
was most distressing, as he was on the very point of 
complying with a dreadful temptation, which would 
have embittered his future life. He had been a disci 
ple of Christ, and was anxious to consecrate his life to 
the service of his adorable Master, and had sought an 
helpmeet to strengthen his hands in serving God. A 
lady, whom he deemed pious, had accepted his ad- 
dresses ; but, when every customary arrangement was 
made, she had dishonorably discarded him. His mind 
was so exceedingly wounded and disgusted, that he 
had determined to choose a wife who made no profes- 
sion of religion, and had fixed on another object for his 
addresses, with every prospect of success, although he 
had not as yet mentioned his intention to her. He 
added, “ But the providence of God led me, an entire 
stranger to this city, to your meeting-house. You may 
suppose that your subject arrested my attention. You 
appeared to be aequainted with every feeling of my 
soul. I saw my danger, and perceived the temptation. 


92 LIFE OF THE 


and the certain ruin of my peace if the dreadful snare 
had not been broken. You, sir, under God, have been 
my deliverer. By the next Sabbath I should have been 
bound in honor to an enemy of that Savior whom I 
adore; for although she is moral and externally ami- 
able, yet she knows not the Savior but by name. I 
could not leave the city in peace until i had sought to 
make this communication.” They unitedly addressed 
him who can deliver, and does deliver his people. 
Thus, whilst part of the congregation thought it an 
unfit subject for the pulpit, others received it as a mes- 
sage from God, by whom it was no doubt sent. 

A lady in high life introduced herself to his study, 
with the apology that she thought it her duty to in- 
form him that, some years before, she was passing the 
street as a stranger, on the Sabbath, when seeing many 
persons enter a passage, she followed them, and found 
herself within his chapel. Every object was new; but 
she listened, and was interested in the sermon. Imme- 
diately after, she left England, and, with some young 
friends, became the inmate of a convent in France, to 
finish their education. While there, every argument 
was employed to convert them to the Roman Catholic - 
religion. Her English associates were overcome by 
these persuasions. “ Your discourse, sir,” said she, 
“which I could never get from my mind, has been 
my preservation from that period to the present, 
though I have been beset with every snare from family 
connections,” &c. Mr. Kilpin recommended suitable 
books to her attention, and devoutly committed her to 
the God of heaven, whilst she knelt with him, bathed 
in tears. Her name or residence he knew not, but we 
may hope, that in glory she also will be recognized 
amongst the numerous host of spiritual children whom 


REY. SAMUEL KILPIN. 93 


the Lord graciously gave him. His study bore witness 
to many such visitors. Similar recitals often cheered 
him. 

It was Mr. Kilpin’s constant endeavor to go from the 
closet to the pulpit. His expression was, “I need to 
have my heart warmed by the Sun of righteousness 
ere I address the hearts of others.” He often remarked, 
“T have preached with self-application to-day, and 
have been humbled in the dust, or have derived divine 
light from the subject presented to view, if no one else 
is benefited.” Frequently he exclaimed, after four or 
five public services on the Sabbath-day, “ Never does 
the blood of Christ appear so valuable as at the close 
of such a Sabbath. In this fountain I bathe. Lord, par- 
don the sins of my holy duties. O how I love thy 
services, my dearest Lord !” 

After hearing a worthy minister preach on the re- 
wards of heaven, he observed, “ That is a doctrine I 
do not understand. My heavenly Father rewards me 
so very amply on earth, in the performance of my poor 
mixed services, that there can be nothing remaining 
as a debt or reward for me, according to my present 
perception of the subject. The work is so exceedingly 
delightful, that I feel grateful to be thus employed.” 
He then dwelt on the word “reward” as dissonant to 
his feelings. He well understood what his Savior’s 
free gifts were, but knew that all his own works re- 
quired pardon, not rewards on earth or in heaven. He 
never preached or spoke on this subject but with these 
feelings. The idea of reward, as it respected God and 
himself, appeared to be obliterated from his mind. 

. After hearing a minister preach as though he was 
determined to win souls to Christ, he observed, “ I en- 
vied my brother to-night. God will surely give him 


94 LIFE OF THE 


souls ; the Holy Ghost has accompanied his preaching. 
O how I wished myself in his place, with his spirit.’ 
Such were his intense desires for souls, that on no- 
ticing in his walks or rides new houses erected, he 
would calculate how many immortal souls each dwell- 
ing was likely to contain, and in what way they might 
be benefited, either by the preached Gospel, prayer, the 
loan of books and Tracts, acts of kindness, &c. &c. 

He often remarked, that if the followers of Christ, 
when on earth, were not converted by his preaching, 
they always forsook the Lord, or gnashed on him with 
their teeth. The Savior did not retain lukewarm con- 
gregations: they were roused to action in some way. 

Mr. Kilpin shared with his brethren in the ministry 
the misery inflicted by anonymous letters. To rid 
himself from these disgraceful epistles, he adopted a 
method which in a few weeks almost entirely prevented 
the recurrence of this evil practice. He published from 
the pulpit his determination never to read a letter that 
had not a signature, except it enclosed a pound-note, 
ora larger sum. Occasionally, some persons who had 
not heard of these regulations, would send remarks to 
which they were ashamed to affix their names. When 
this occurred, Mr. Kilpin would mention the circum- 
stance publicly, and again state that all anonymous 
epistles were burnt as soon as it was discovered that 
the requisite remuneration for reading was not en- 
closed ! 


We now return to his domestic labors. 

In the year 1826, two objects engaged much of Mr 
Kilpin’s thoughts and prayers. One was, an institu- 
tion for the reception and education of the children of 
poor ministers of different denominations—the orphan 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 95 


families of missionaries were particularly included in 
- these wishes. His design was not to permit the exclu- 
sion of any denomination. He sent a letter to differ- 
ent magazines, requesting advice, plans, &c. but it was 
admitted only into one. From personal friends and 
various ministers he also sought direction; but no 
efforts, however varied, were successful. They did not 
produce one encouraging letter on the subject, although 
his plans were partly «arranged, and provision was 
made in some measure for a commencement. For ten 
years this subject had rested on his mind. This was 
the only thing he attempted which totally failed. 

The other object was the establishment of an orphan- 
house for female children. Both these institutions 
were presented before the throne of grace, waiting the 
Divine decision. The first was rejected—the last ac- 
cepted, and the way made so exceedingly clear, that 
unbelief could not raise an obstacle. The history of 
this institution was very remarkable, and the interpo- 
sition of Providence appeared as striking, on a smaller 
scale, as in Professor Frank’s establishment at Halle, 
in Germany, or in the American Orphan-house. To 
God it wholly belongs. He was its Founder, Patron, 
and Treasurer. If its history were written, it ought 
to convince even an infidel, that verily there is a God 
who heareth prayer. The Orphan-house is situated 
in the garden, a few yards from the cottage where he 
resided; and Mr. Kilpin lived to see thirty-six admit- 
fed asinmates. He was exceedingly fond of the chil- 
dren, and they were equally attached to him, 


The following letter to a friend, shows the value of 
the Refuge for Orphans. It was written after the re 
fuge had been open about eighteen months: 


96 LIFE OF THE 


“Our daily visits often bring us into contact with 
circumstances overwhelming and painful, but not al-« 
ways fit for publication. One lately occurred, which 
I can and must mention. A man and his wife, honest 
laborers, and of regular habits, had ten children. After 
tugging and striving, and always disdaining a parish 
allowance, at last they broke under the weight of their 
family cares. ‘The mother first gave way, at forty- 
two years of age: the father, poor man, still strove, 
but his spirits could not bear up—weakness and the 
dropsy took him offsoon after. Eight orphans were 
left unprovided for; one only eight months old, the 
eldest aged seventeen. Whose heart felt their woes? 
A sister, herself left a widow with five children, who 
keeps a little shop, but much more likely to be cheated 
out of five shillings a week than to clear two—she 
would have them; and then you might have seen thir- 
teen orphans asleep in two rooms, and only a widow 
that would care for them ! 

“She was recommended to take them to the work- 
house. ‘No, not my children,’ said she, ‘nor my bro- 
ther’s. No, not if bread can be got.’ The God of 
orphans saw her efforts. The parish allowed her 
eighteen pence a week for five of those under fourteen ; 
a friend took the girl of seventeen as a servant; the 
elder boy, though lame, will strive to spare a little out 
of his poor pittance as a country tailor. But what is 
the girl of fourteen todo? The parish would do no- 
thing for her but bind her apprentice, almost to her 
certain ruin. An orphan’s friend brought her up to 
our house: Moses in the bulrushes looked not more 
interesting. She stood firm and upright, her head a 
little bent, an expressive countenance, while she told 
her own tale of ‘mother’s death’ and ‘father’s sor- 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 97 


rows,’ and how she tried to ease his pains by rubbing 
his limbs—while she spoke she wept. I never wit- 
nessed such simple, yet such perfect eloquence. ‘And 
can you take me in, sir? Ican read and sew, I am 
willing to do any thing.’ Poor.girl! we were willing 
before she asked. ‘Do you fear God? ‘Hope so, sir.’ 
‘Have you been used to spend the Sabbath in idleness 
when mother and father were living? ‘No, hope not, 
sir; we always, all of us, went twice a-day to our 
church. No, sir; never wished to break the Sabbath.’ 

“This was enough—we told her to come into the 
house appointed for just such individuals as she was. 
I begged that the aunt might bring her; I longed to 
see the aunt. This afternoon she came, and I felt a 
veneration for her I cannot describe. I thought, What 
am I, compared to this poor woman? What did I 
ever venture, compared with her? I felt like a brute 
beast before my God, and I thought she must appear 
angelic. The poor girl isin the Orphan Refuge, and 
we hope to prepare her for service. I said to her, 
‘ Have you a Bible, child? Her countenance changed 
a little; trembling, and as if ashamed, she answered, 
‘No, sir, not a Bible; I had one, but gave it to my 
brother ; but I have a little Testament.’ She has been 
with us a week, and we have every prospect of her be- 
ing a good girl. We now have nine in the Refuge; 
we have had nineteen in all, and know nothing bad of 
any. I think we may say that six are truly praying 
souls.” 


Would that similar institutions abounded in every 
part of our land! When arranged on the simple plan 
adopted by Mr. and Mrs. Kiipin, they are neither diffi- 


eult to conduct, nor require large funds; the main 
9 Kilpin. 


98 LIFE OF THE 


point is, judicious and efficient personal superintend- 
ence by those who are interested for the work. 

The missionary societies in general had his warm 
support. For sailors he delighted to plead. In the last 
sermon he preached, he strongly and affectingly re- 
commended the sailors’ cause with tears. In fact, there 
was not any branch of benevolence in which he was 
not engaged, nor any religious society which he had 
not assisted in some way. His soul was in constant 
exercise; every subject connected with-the extension 
of the Redeemer’s kingdom excited lively emotion and 
effort. The record is on high. 

Some other letters, written during the later years of 
Mr. Kilpin’s life, may now be inserted. 


On the Disposal of Property. 


“Dear Sir,—The property of this world is intimate. 
ly connected with the operations of the Holy Spirit. 
David said: ‘Out of my poverty have I given to the 
Lord.’ It was a thousand talents of gold! Probabty 
you will ask, what proportion of our property ought 
we to devote to God and his cause. I say, all that is 
not contradicted by the precepts of God—his laws 
never cross each other. If he bid me love my neigh- 
bors as myself, he does not mean that I should love 
one to the neglect of another, or reduce myself to po- 
verty; this would be loving them better than myself 
or my family, and bringing me to the verge of pau- 
perism, in which I may at last become dependent on 
the hard-earned wages of the poor man for sustenance, 
and so degrade that religion I wish to honor. The 
question may return, What should you suppose ascrip- 
tural portion? I should say, in all common cases, a 


REY. SAMUEL KILPIN. 99 


tenth of the whole you possess. There may be extra 
cases when a free-will offering may call for more, and 
the liberal soul would feel a luxury in giving the last 
farthing, and running into the next quarter, if need re- 
quired. Here prudence and piety must dictate. I do 
not believe our dear Lord calls for more in his Gospel. 
There may be an occasion when a widow may throw 
in all she has—but always to do so would not be honest. 
A case may be easily supposed at any of our public 
collections, when a nobleman might throw into the 
plate a hundred pounds, and a poor widow only a pen- 
ny, the whole she had. In the balances of eternity we 
know which would appear the greatest act of benevo- 
lence ; but, if the nobleman was to throw in all he pos- 
sessed, he would be dishonest, perhaps, to ten thou- 
sand creditors, or those who had lawful claims upon 
him, and throw into confusion many families that 
could not wait the annual return of his resources, but 
would, in the intermediate period, be dishonest to their 
creditors, and bring ruin on themselves, 
“ Yours, indeed, S. Ke? 


The following letter, occasioned by the decease o1 
one of his sisters, is an illustration of the kindly affec- 
tions of his heart, and how he was enabled to lay them 
at his Master’s feet: 


“My pear Sisrer,—Our beloved sister has fallen 
asleep in the arms of her and our Beloved. We are 
stunned, yet there is nothing deranged. He that go- 
verns the world hath made no mistake: there are no 
links broken, no confusion: it was the appointed time 
of our heavenly Father. All is well. If the provi 
dence of God had removed our dear sister to any part 


100 LIFE OF THE 


of England, Ireland, Wales, nay, to America, by the 
possession of an estate or property, we perhaps should 
not have murmured. Very likely some of the younger 
branches would have been solicitous to accompany 
her. We might have regretted to part, but should often 
talk about the plan, and anticipate the pleasure of meet- 
ing again. And now shall we repine? Has heaven 
no charms? Do we not exclaim: ‘O, my Father, let 
me go next? Are there none from Bedford, Newport, 
Exeter, soon to follow her? Has the world more fas- 
cinating charms than Jesus? Then do we hail the 
spirits of the just made perfect—father, mother, sister, 
brother—these parts of ourselves who are in heaven, 
as safe, as honorable, happy, and glorious; and do we 
fear lest we should be the next to be made so safe, so 
honorable, so happy, so glorious? If Gabriel were to 
touch our arms this evening, and say, ‘The Master is 
come, and calleth for thee,’ would any one say, ‘We 
would rather follow the dead body of our sister, than 
her pure and blessed spirit? O! my dear brother and 
sister, the religion of Paul was not such: he felt a 
burning thirst to be with Christ, which is far better: 
John Ryland said, ‘Paltry world, it is infinitely better? 
May we be all ready to join the halleluiahs above.” 


The following was written to a friend, when antici- 
pating the removal of a beloved infant, and shows the 
strong sympathy of Mr. Kilpin’s feelings on such a 

Subject: ' 


“Your letter much affected me, and I painfully an- 
ticipate that the desire of your eyes is taken away! 1° 
am glad you can anticipate it with so much right feel- 
ing, and with such right thoughts. But when the stroke 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 101 


comes— when the dear countenance turns pale—when 
the sweet lips have lost their rubies—when the spark- 
ling eyes are closed—when death has showed his pow- 
er—when sin has proved its existence—you will feel a 
sensation indescribable; a yearning of soul, the bare 
mention of which sets my whole frame in a state of 
agitation I cannot describe. Yet you will have strength, 
not only to bear it, but to glorify your God. And so 
will also the esteemed mother: but her heart will feel 
the bursting of these ligaments more distracting, more 
painful, than all the pangs of nature; yet they will 
subside, and some sweet views by faith, guided by re- 
velation, views of Him that said, ‘Suffer little children 
to come unto me,’ will compose the mind, and she will 
say, ‘Itisthe Lord.’ Millions of infant souls compose 
the family above. But why dolIso write? Truly be- 
cause I love you, and feel instantly when any thing 
like death appears upon children. It is the sore, the 
tender place with me, the place of exquisite feeling. 
If I read in the papers of the deaths of children, or of 
youths, it sends me immediately to a throne of mercy 
for their poor distressed parents. 

“T was glad to see that you wished not the Divine 
Being to go out of his own way, but to visit you as he 
used to do to them that fear him. I knew a case, in 
which the minister, praying over a child apparently 
dying, said, ‘If it be thy will, spare—.” The poor 
mother’s soul, yearning for her beloved, exclaimed— 
‘It must be his will, I cannot bear ifs.’ The minister 
stopped. To thesurprise of many, the child recovered ; 
and the mother, after almost suffering martyrdom by 
him while a stripling, lived to see him hanged before 
he was two-and-twenty! O! it is good to say, Not my 


will, but thine.”

From a letter to his Niece. 


‘Well, my dear Mary, I got home, to my dear home, 
and to my dearer E. I caught cold, but am now much 
better. Iam getting into regular habits, and begin to 
gather some food for my soul, feeling much pleasure 
in drawing nigh to God. It is good to draw near ;all 
things else are but little in religion. Religion, without 
communion, is but a bubble to deceive—a chain of de- 
lusive gold; the more it resembles reality, the more 
dangerous. Our great enemy, my dear M., and our 
desperately wicked hearts, care not how much like real 
religion we have, if it be not real, not spiritual. To 
be spiritually-minded is life and peace. Stop not short 
of this, my beloved M.; hold communion with God, 
and you are safe. It will teach you all things; it will 
give you a sudden thought, and call to remembrance a 
sentence or a promise. You shail hear it in the street, 
in the gardens, in the fields, in your room, in your 
heart. O walk with God and you will never err. 
Whilst you ought to be friendly, because you have 
many dear friends, yet be much alone. I cannot con- 
ceive how some professors live; they appear always 
desirous of being in company. O sweet retirement! 
I frequently do not see your aunt for many hours. I 
had rather be in my study eleven hours in the day, than 
be out of it one. It is impossible to serve God or our 
own souls without time. What is the world, or its 
pleasures, or its prospects, or its beauties, without our 
God? There is so much sameness in every thing, after 
having seen it once or twice, that we feel but little in- 
terest, if it be merely of the world, though ever so mo- 
ral or refined in its nature. But retirement, sweet so- 
litude, blessed books, a gracious Savior—O there! 
there! there are the luxuries of souls. May my dar- 


REY. SAMUEL KILPIN. 103 


ling Mary have ten thousand more pleasures in such 
things than her uncle has; and he would not change 
house, or wife, or friends, or books, or study, with all 
the great or good men onearth. I have seen great 
things and fine things, good things and good men, but 
I have seen nothing equal, from Exeter to Liverpool. 
No; here I retire; the world is excluded and God is 
enjoyed. Here I sit, and pray, and plan; here I receive 
the strongest tokens of divine favor; here I behold his 
hands, his feet, his head, his side, his heart, his love, 
and have no doubts about my interest. Asa pardoned 
sinner, I labor, as his servant, to convert souls. Many 
professors of religion disturb me by their idleness, in- 
difference, coldness, and this often for fear they are 
strangers to Christ! Then again I see souls weeping, 
and coming to him; then all is on fire. Last Sabbath 
week I felt as much as my body could bear in the pul- 
pit. Come, my dear Mary, give yourself wholly to the 
Lord: be not afraid; only get his grace and spend it. 
Be sure you are right, and never think of going to 
heaven without taking some persons with you. 

“Let me have a letter from you. Tell me how 
much you love me; I shall like to hear it: but, O tell 
me how you love Him who loved us, died for us, lived 
for us, was born for us—love his cause, his kingdom! 
I had rather you would hate me with perfect hatred, 
than love our dear Lord with only the same affection 
as you have for me. The Lord bless you. Thank my 
dear sister for all her kind love. The Lord bless her. 
Love to dear M.; tell him to be hearty in the cause of 
God, and to live near to God. Tell him not to be taken 
with the common chit chat of religion. We are all 
religious in our day! all going to hear! Religiow is a 
trade, a hobby, an amusement, a plaything ix silver
