slippers and golden chains. Most people go to hear 
the minister, to talk about the minister :—‘ O, he is fine, 
sweet, delightful, wonderful, clever, &c.’ Ah! but 
where is Christ? where is conscience? experience? 
spirituality ?” 


Mr. Kilpin’s bodily strength now was considerably 
enfeebled, but as yet he was not aware how much the 
general system was undermined. Several circum- 
stances also united to try him. In 1826 he thus wrote 
to a friend: 


“'The last six weeks have been a time of trial every 
way ; a trial of bodily health, of mind, a trial of grace 
and of graces; a time of great wrestling with God, of 
great temptation from the enemy. The world has 
lessened much-in my estimation; and if Iam not sore- 
ly deceived in my own heart, I have not love of life 
enough to ask its continuance one hour, but for His 
service and the promotion of his cause. Our work 
multiplies on us, and the blessing of God evidently at- 
tends in each place of labor. Many souls are inquiring 
the way to heaven. We are heavily laden, and sail 
deep in water, but sail well against wind and tide. 
Preaching is health to me, and is my physician.” 


In another letter he says : 


“Our labors increase on every hand, and succeed. 
"hrough grace, we triumph in Jesus Christ. We have 
many difficulties, many trials. I feel that the constant 
exercise of nerve and mind will lead to seventy years 
of age before I am sixty.” 


After inentioning some of the trials, he proceeds: 


REY. SAMUEL KILPIN. 105 
; 


“ A few more such certainly will endanger my life, 
Out hitherto the Lord has caused us to triumph. Never 
did I conceive it possible that a female could be of the 
consequence my dear Eliza has been to me.” 


Not long after, he writes: 


“We are truly engaged wholly for God and his ser- 
vice, nor do I think Mrs. K. or myself would wish to 
live for any purpose short of God’s immediate service. 
We have many trials, which, if reckoned, might give 
a gloom to the mind, but we have no time to calculate 
them, and our mercies are innumerable. Living by 
faith is killing to the flesh and exhausting to the body 
but good for the soul.” 


It is unnecessary, and would not be desirable here, 
to enter into the particulars which constituted or ag- 
gravated these trials. Some were local, others were 
constitutional, and if any were from unkindness, either 
intentional or that might have been avoided, the suf- 
ferer has long since forgiven those who inflicted them. 
As he said in another letter, “I have a little too much 
to bear from all quarters, and feel a little too much.” 


Early in 1828, Mr. Kilpin’s health and strength were 
so far exhausted that medical advice was considered 
necessary. His physician considered that as yet his 
illness proceeded only from disordered functions, but 
warned him that it was “necessary to give immediate 
and serious attention to his complaint.” He attributed 
the origin entirely to Mr. Kilpin’s “ indefatigable and 
long-continued ministerial labors,” and that his health 
had so far been providentially supported; for “none 


106 LIFE OF THE 


but a most vigorous constitution could have escaped 
without irreparable injury.” Complete repose was 
pointed out as necessary, adding, “ Change of air and 
regular exercise will facilitate, but mental relaxation 
is absolutely necessary to your recovery.” In remark- 
ing upon this opinion, Mr. Kilpin speaks of recent cir- 
cumstances having been a sore trial to him; and says, 
“but through great mercy there is nothing in the way 
upward—the heavens are cloudless. I have found in 
our own atmosphere some clouds or hazy days, but 
nothing between God my Savior. and my own soul. 
Indeed, my affliction has been attended with great self- 
examination and nearness of the soul to God.” 


He retired to Teignmouth for a brief interval of re- 
pose. Writing from thence, in April, he says: 


“He who sees we spend all we have of body, mind, 
and substance, in his service, replenishes in a way 
that justifies the venture of faith which by some is 
called enthusiasm. My mind is now made up to retire 
from every thing like iabor for eight weeks, but I have 
had as many strokes to let the yoke be pulled off, as 
the slave undergoes to have it put on. O it has been 
a sore trial; but there are few trials in life, when our 
wills are absorded in the Divine will. Submit, and no 
burden but is bearable, and often delightful. It isa 
mercy to have a soul like a weaned child ; but there is 
a good deal of weeping and restlessness before a child 
is weaned I think my soul is so now, but it has made 
me look ten years older 

“YT have no doubt it isa crisis of my life, and events 
are ready, or nearly so, to show my path, either out 
to sea again, or to the land of my fathers! Blessed 


REV. SAMUEL KILPLN. 107 


eountry! There is collected a number that no man 
can number. Thanks be to God, the employment is 
the same here as there. What is it for them to live, 
but for Christ? What for us? Afflictions take off the 
imposing glare that the things of this life present. If 
sanctified, they put things into their places, and then, 
eompared with that which is spiritual, they are but 
little worth, and, in the scale of eternity, only as the 
drop of the bucket.” 


Dropsical symptoms now came on rapidly, and 
threatened immediate dissolution. Two skilful medi 
cal men were called in, and pronounced the disease 
to be most alarming. They advised Mr. Kilpin to re- 
turn home. The journey produced relief. His legs, 
which were greatly swelled, burst. He was benefited 
by the discharge, but suffered intense pain from a large 
wound which ensued. Speaking of it, he says, ‘ This 
makes me almost faint, and I take an hour to dress. 
Pray for me, dear friend. O how I want patience! 
O how incapable I am at times to read or pray, or do 
any thing! It appears to drink up my spirits and my 
mercies, and refuses to be healed. But it came as my 
physician; I have consulted Him that sent it, and he 
kindly answered me that he wounded and he healed, 
and invited me to leave all with him. He kindly says, 
Leave it allto me. With bitter tears I agreed, if he 
would give me strength to bear the pain, and not let 
me be impatient. And when I do leave it, I have pa- 
tience, but often spring up, and think a dog is biting 
me. ThusI amacomplete prisoner. I can see much 
mercy, and one great end the Lord has in view in thus 
holding me. But, my very dear friend, do pray for 
me—it is a sore trial.” 


198 LIFE OF THE 


The shock was so severe that the constitution coulu 
not be rallied, although death was retarded. Notwith- 
standing his sufferings were severe, he preached occa- 
sionally. 

The following letter was written during these suf- 
ferings : 


To Miss B—, Liverpool. July 28, 1828. 


“My very pear Mary,—Your letter of the Sth I re- 
ceived with no common sensation. Indeed, my dearest 
Mary, I have wept over it; and prayed over it, and fre- © 
quently repeated a sentiment prominent in it. To come 
out of such a furnace, so much more heated than usual, 
without much increase of humility and holiness, would 
be a horrible thing. Thank you, my dearest girl, for 
the expressions of solicitude and love; they again 
make me weep with pleasure, and gratitude to God, 
that Ihave sucha niece. Your dear aunt ioves you in- 
creasingly for it. 

“My sufferings have been great, and death has been 
apparently near; but since my recovery from the drop- 
sy, of which no trace is left, the pain in my legs has 
been more acute. Long tedious hours—before I was 
so ill, I used to lie awake by night, and pray our dear 
Lord to remember your dearly-beloved father in his 
agonies, and dear sister in her anxieties, and you in 
your strong affectionate feelings for so beloved a fa- 
ther; and even in my own agonies I have frequently 
exclaimed, What are these pains to those of my dear 
brother B—? Yet they have been very sore to me, a 
bullock unaccustomed to such a yoke; but I never 
murmured once, but stood like a child, weeping and 
praying, night and day. Many sweet manifestations, 
my dear Mary, have I had; many portions of Scrip 


REV. SAMUEL KILPiN. 109 


ture remarkabiy suitable have been brought to my 
mind. Never did I see the beauty of the Bible so 
clearly, nor do I recollect ever having more simple 
faith in what it declares, Isaiah, 30:15, ‘For thus saith: 
the Lord God, the Holy One of Israel; in returning 
and rest shall ye be saved; in quietness and con- 
fidence shall be your strength: and ye would not.’ 
Cutting off the last sentence, ‘and ye would not,’ this 
text has been, and is now, an unspeakable delight. The 
sacrifice of our Lord Jesus Christ, for the justification 
ef our covenant God in showing mercy, and for the 
justification:.of @ poor sinner, appeared with unspeak- 
able splendor; and the taking him at his word, ‘Him 
that cometh unto me, I will in no wise cast out,’ kept 
me in perfect peace, as it respected my future state. 
The character of God, as God, the character of Christ, 
es Mediator, the operations of the Holy Ghost, were 
subjects of adoration and praise. What God was, I 
wished to be; what the Savior is, was all my hope; and 
the blessed testimony that this was my heart’s experi- 
ence, by the Holy Spirit, kept me from any doubts. I 
had many painful, very painful conflicts; the enemy 
shot at me, and wounded me; but my Rock he reached 
not, nor me in that Rock. If the Bible is true, I am 
right for both worlds. My spirits at times are equal 
to'any thing, but a very little exertion tells me that in 
-quietness and confidence is my strength. 

“Your aunt unites with the strongest affection to 
you, and to dear M——, and father and mother. Be- 
iieve me, my very dearest ih gare vce affectionate 
uncle.”

enfeebled state, the long journey from Exeter to Bed-

was given to him; and by the rapid and easy methods 
of traveling now generally introduced, he reached his

tour to Liverpool and Birmingham. The following 
memorandum was written during this excursion : 


“ August.—After a journey of mercy, reached New- 
port Pagnel, and erected our Ebenezer in love and 
peace. Sabbath,—kept at home by the weather ; look- 
ed at my diary; ought to have hinted at my sore temp- 
tations when ill, and the placid state of my mind as it 
respects another world. Through my afflictions, a 
simplicity of faith on our Lord Jesus Christ has been 
granted to me on what he has done and suffered, was 
doing in heaven, and will do, for every poor coming 
sinner: this was my unshaken confidence, on which, 
through mercy, the enemy never made one attack. I 
had no personal doubts of interest inthe Savior. Ihave 
but one reason I can assign, which, I think, has been 
the means of this peace; namely, that I always went 
as a poor destitute sinner to our Lord Jesus Christ, as 
the only, unmixed, Savior; not immediately saying 
that he died for me, but in the assurance that he died 
for sinners, and for every sinner that casts himself on 
him, and ventures on him as his complete Redeemer, 
in which the character of God is more glorified by a 
sinner’s salvation through Jesus Christ’s sacrifice, than 
it could ever be in their eternal punishment. 

“Reached Birmingham.—Sweetly presented my 
evening sacrifice to God. Some cheering ideas con- 
veyed t6 my mind, yet always find sin mixed with all: 
boo.”

The following extract from a letter describes some 
of his sufferings during the period just noticed: 


“The last year has been a year of sore trial, by af- 
fliction of body. My strength and spirits gave way a 
year and a half ago, but Dawlish and Teignmouth ral- 
lied them again. There was something not right again 
about December, and last January a tremendous cough 
and cold sorely tried me. A visit to Torquay, about 
this time, relieved the cough; but it was renewed again, 
and all things appeared to be against my general health, 
until my legs burst, and became drains. The water in 
two hours would fill my shoes, and run out in a large 
pool on the floor. Life then seemed fast running out 
The blisters became ulcers, and for many, many nights 
and days, they were sore things indeed. One became 
mortified, and it was uncertain many days whether 
the leg must not be amputated. But the Lord gave 
the word, and the whole of that part came out, and 
gave a change to the scene. The wounds did not heal 
until the latter end of November. The exhaustions I 
suffered were dreadful. I was literally drenched with 
port wine by day and night. My good doctor attend- 
ed me nearly the whole year; through mercy, all be- 
yond the body and the world was perfectly calm. Isai- 
ah, 30:15, ‘For thus saith the Lord God, the Holy 
One of Israel, in returning and rest shall ye be saved ; 
and in quietness and confidence shall be your strength,’ 
was a continued support. It was a great mercy that 
all things were settled daily. I do not know that for 
many years I ever closed my eyes in sleep with an 
unsettled account with God. The atonement which 
oar dear Lord made was the only hope. He is my 
Fiigh Priest and Refuge; to him I simply fled: and as 


112 LIFE OF THE 


‘ they of old time were always safe, until the death of 
the high priest, in an earthly refuge; so, until he dies 
in the heavenly, I expect to be safe—and he liveth for 
ever. But I was sorely harassed at times with strong 
conflicts. IL rather suppose the enemy thought the 
time was short. I think this affliction was partly sent 
to make me willing to loosen from South-street. I 
therefore have proposed an assistant, and he has been 
unanimously chosen. Iam now increasing in strength 
daily; you would not suppose any thing had been the 
matter, if in my company; and I wish you were. i 
can stand an hour’s work well; but though I appear 
more free from cough than for ten years past, my firm 
strength is not yet come.” 


In November he returned with healed legs, and so 
much improved in health as in part to resume his pas- 
toral duties. He says, “ Memorandum—The first Sab- 
bath after my journey home, preached with as much 
ease as I recollect for forty years. Preached again in 
the evening, but it is a little more than I can do with 
firmness at present. Better in quietness and confidence 
to wait a little longer.” 

He immediately acted on a resolution formed at Li- 
verpool whilst hearing a sermon by Dr. Marshman, 
and procured an acceptable and efficient assistant, with 
whom he united harmoniously, and shared the labors 
of the ministry for a few months. But finding that a 
general lassitude and exhaustion followed the efforts 
of preaching—for when in the pulpit, his body was 
forgotten in the exercises of his soul—after many 
struggles, he decided to resign the office of pastor over 
his beloved flock, with his usual simplicity. He as- 
signed his reasons for thus acting: “I now retain the 


REY. SAMUEL KILPIN. 113 


office of pastor, which brings with it a responsibility 
that no language can express. Iam incapable of ful- 
filling its duties from disease. I receive a small sum 
connected with this name, for which I must give an 
account on the other side Jordan. However trifling 
the amount, it comes from the appointment of those 
who reside not in this world, and who never intended 
to leave their money for religious sinecures. God is 
the guardian of all such legacies, and wo be to those 
who abuse them. My assistant brother is competent 
to fill the situation alone, and I could peaceably and 
honorably resign my charge, under God, tohim, Into 
my heavenly Father’s hands I commit the solemn trust, 
and in his strength, relying on his word, I will enter 
another bishopric, which does not possess one farthing 
as a fund or income. Yet my Savior is the Head of 
the church, and he never starves his laborers. His 
heart and purse are large.” 

The next Sabbath he presided at the Lord’s table, 
and with the most intense feelings of affection ad- 
dressed his beloved flock. He loved them as the re- 
deemed of the Lord, many of them as the spiritual 
children whom the Lord had given him for his hire. 
His soul yearned over them. He agonized with God 
for their spiritual prosperity and peace as individuals; 
and as a church, no exertion, no sacrifice: was spared 
to promote its welfare. He was in seasoi and out of 
season. - 

An anecdote of his pastoral conduct will elucidate 
his character. On the divisions of the paper which 
covers his parlor study, every pew in the chapel was 
marked with the names, in short-hand, of their oveu- 
pants, lest one should be forgotten. Here he daily 
presented his petitions to God for the spiritual pros- 

10* 


“114 LIFE OF THE 


perity of each dear object, according to their various 
situations and wants, as far as he knew their charac- 
ters, temptations, and trials. Many of these pastoral 
marks still remain. ; 

The church were not aware of the event that await- 
edthem. Mr. Kilpin and his wife remarked that they 
had never partaken of the Lord’s supper with more 
delightful feelings of union and fellowship with every 
member, nor looked at each with more pleasure and 
sweet anticipation of meéting at the marriage-supper 
of the Lamb. On the next night the resignation was 
read, recommending the church to elect Mr. B——, 
and all to rally around him, and strengthen his hands. 

The following letter shows his feelings at this pain- 
ful period: 


To Mrs. S. B., Teignmouth. January 30th, 1829. 


“My DEAR GRANDMAMMA,— What mercies you have 
seen! Once more you have seen your dear children; 
once more the Lord has raised you up from the borders 
of the grave. I do not know any body, except myself, 
that has more reason to bless and adore our dear Lord 
Jesus Christ than you have. Trust cheerfully in him. 
He has taken care of you before you loved him, since 
you have been one of his widows, and more than as 
long again as he took care of the Israelites in the wil- 
derness. He has supplied all your wants, so that you 
have lacked nothing in this great, long, howling wil- 
derness ; and now you are coming up out of it leaning 
on your beloved Lord. Lean on him hard: bear your 
whole weight, body and soul, on him, for time and for 
eternity, for living moments and for dying ones too. 
Read Isaiah, 30.. There are some sweet soul-cheering 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. Ti 


words. I live on these every day: ‘In quietness and 
confidence is your strength.’ Keep looking, that is, 
expecting, the mercy of our God through Jesus Christ 
our Lord unto eternal life. Expect nothing but mer- 
ey, and you will please God, and he will take pleasure 
in you. O what sweet words are these—‘He taketh 
pleasure in them that fear him, in them that hope in 
his mercy.” Do not fear death, do not fear dying. 
‘Fear not,’ says our Lord, ‘for Iam with thee.” Lay 
hold of that one letter, I—‘I am with thee.’ 


Immediately Mr. Kilpin commenced enlarging and 
farther fitting up his Garden, or Refuge Chapel. He 
added a gallery, and erected pews. Mr. Kilpin’s lan- 
guage was, O Lord, give me to gather a church from 
the world, send whom thou wilt send. He did not 
know of one individual that would unite with them, or 
take a sitting as part of the congregation. He looked 
to the Savior alone. 


From a memorandum. “April 29th, 1829. The 
chapel was opened. This is a new era in my life. I 
have been brought near to death. I never was con- 
fined to my bed but one day. Though fifty-five years 
old, if I may be useful a few years longer, here I am. 
Do with me, O Lord, what seemeth good in thy sight. 
Amen.” 

“Since I have written the above, it has almost mira- 
culously appeared that the: first sermon was sent of 
God, and by God, to two souls. Halleluiah!” 


There is no doubt but the divine blessing was vouch- 
safed to these proceedings. Before long there was 
good evidence that several souls were converted. The 


116 LIFE OF THE 


congregations were very good, composed generally of 
strangers. Several very useful members relieved Mr. 
Kilpin from much anxiety relative to visiting the sick, 
&c. The Sabbath-schools continued to be numerously 
attended, and every thing appeared to prosper; the 
blessing of God was visibly granted, and his presence 
was felt. One erection of pews followed another. Mr. 
Kilpin saw four enlargements; all was harmony and 
peace ; every department of labor resounded with prais- 
es; the desire of the invalid’s heart was granted to its 
utmost extent. His illness, although much mitigated, 
still continued severe, and reminded him of the frail 
tenure of all earthly things. Thus things proceeded 
for several months. Mr. Kilpin was enabled to preach 
two or three times every Sabbath, and occasionally in 
the week. Never was a sweeter prospect presented to 
a minister’s view—not a desire unfulfilled. The God 
of peace and tenderness gave much indulgence and 
spiritual luxury to his enfeebled servant. His soul 
was overwhelmed with the lagen kindness and tender 
mercies of his God. 

The Savior sojourned upon earth to save sinners, 
and angels would gladly be employed on such a de- 
lightfulerrand. Mr. Kilpin always said that he could 
never wish or ask for life for other purposes, nor could 
he feel eager for death whilst thus employed. But he 
always entreated that his labors for God and his life 
might cease together: this request was granted. 

Symptoms of dropsy began again to appear; the 
legs again burst, and wounds of the most frightful de- 
scription now followed. A square yard of plaster was 
daily used in dressing them. His last year’s sufferings 
commenced, and very dreadful was his affliction. It 
disabled him from walking—he was drawn from room 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 117 


to room. A Bath chair conveyed him to his beloved 
chapel, where he often preached amidst great weakness. 


In a letter, June 13th, 1829, he says: 


“The congregation are, three parts of them, new 
faces, out of this neighborhood. I have openly refused 
members of other churches, declaring that my inten- 
tion and prayers were directed to the quarry of nature, 
by the power of the God of Abraham, from its stones 
to raise children to him. Our Sabbath school amounts 
toa hundred. Nine sweet orphans. The last room of 
the house is nearly finished. Our girls’ and infant 
daily schools are working well. But, ah me! the boys’ 
school I have been obliged to give up. I offered to 
educate sixty boys annually, and superintend it my- 
self, if forty pounds were raised ; but not one subscriber 
came forward. Last year I was minus twenty-five 
pounds on it. Many times have I given my last shil- 
ling on a Saturday to make up the weekly pay. It 
preyed on my spirits; I found it would stop every 
wheel, and render the whole station miserable. A cir- 
ecumstance occurred which decided me. It stopped. 
As we always pay weekly, there were no debts to be 
demanded. Till God enables us to raise the means 
from our own resources, it must lay by. The boys’ 
-school-room is now filled with children from three to 
nine years old. It has borne some lovely fruit, in the 
conversion of, I trust, six children and parents. Our 
minds feel more than usually dependent, simply de- 
pendent on our Lord Jesus Christ—weaned by sor- 
rows ard tears, and enchanted by displays of redeem- 
ing grace, the free grace of our Savior, and without 
a wish to live for any other purpose than his glory. I 


118 LIFE OF THE 


reckon the last two years, especially the last year of 
affliction, trial, and sorrow, the best, the most delight- 
ful for the health of the soul that I have ever known; 
the mercies of God have been so apparent, so nume- 
rous, so almost miraculous, so overpowering, so con- 
vincing of his love, so fully proving that we are his, 
and he is ours, that it almost crushes the body with 
the weight of gratitude we feel. Yet I have been sorely 
tried with temptations. Satan has fought inch by inch. 
My own heart has had such awful displays of its own 
vileness, that had it not been for the manifestations ot 
the Spirit, exhibiting the glory of Christ in his glori- 
ous perfections and perfectly finished work, my mind 
must have sunk under them, or I should have gone to 
the peaceful clods of the valley. Blessed, blessed, 
thrice blessed be his name, he has always caused me 
to triumph in him !” 


To Mr. —. Sept. 10, 1829. 


“My ppar Brotuer,—If you were to come to Exeter 
now, you would find things much changed, but I hope 
quite as well as usual, and in many respects better. It 
was a sore trial to leave, but afflictions made it abso-

dear little Bethel, Refuge Chapel, would delight you. 
Two-thirds are pewed, and a gallery crammed full 
with children. A good congregation in the morning, 
full in the afternoon, and running over at night. I 
often do not know thirty of the congregation. This is 
what we wish.

closes to us. All must remain in South-street, and help

“ Last Sabbath-day was our ordinance; we mustered

We had a most heavenly season. Our prayers are an- 
swering. Lord! let the little one become a thousand 
Pray for this little spot enclosed by grace. The day

more might be done, but at present we have not

Yet the fountain that is opened (Zech. 13: 1,) is never

1. “I never think of any reward beside the delight 
in the work. I may distinctly say, that such a thought 
never enters to animate me; but it is simply the glory 
of God my Savior, and the intrinsic excellency of the 
work itself. 

2. “I never work under any impression that I can 
accomplish any thing; or that the sinner or saint will 
ever be moved to do any thing by what I say; but I 
most firmly believe that I am God’s servant, and only 
a servant; I look to him as the Master, and work, ex- 
pecting that he will accomplish the purposes of his

serious impressions now, as at any time of my : 


To a Friend. 


“ Dear Sir,—I have some painful tokens of relapse 
sf dropsy; my legs are much swollen. I hope all is 
surrendered into His hands who manages all things 
well; but not to be able to spring about, or take any 
exercise, except in a coach, is so unlike former days 
that it isa sore trial; yet our mercies are innumerable. 
I do not know any period of our lives. when more has 
been actually accomplished ‘in one year. It has been 
a year indeed of trial, yet of unspeakable, unaccount- 
able mercy. All we have in hand, especially the chapel 
and the church, succeeds; the little one bids fair to be- 
come a thousand ; and I do confess I should like to live 
to see it. 

“ As to my first impressions relating to the ministry : 
when young, such was the horror of my mind, and so 
determined was I not to be a minister, that my anxiety 
brought on a weakness in my stomach, which refused 
to retain any thing I had eaten during one year, and 
reduced me almost to a shadow. Hours have I spent 
in the fields, under hedges, by night and by day, pray- 
ing that the great God would not send me into the 
ministry, but deliver me from the delusions of the 
devil; but the Lord wonderfully brought me out, forced 
me out, and wonderfully has he helped me, and kept 
me to this hour; to him be all honor and praise, for 
ever and ever. I was a pastor fourteen years at Leo- 
minster; more than s‘xteen years have I sustained the 
office in this city. I have worked hard, and suffered 
much contradiction of sinners, and of professors, 
against myself, and have sunk—I recall the word— 
not sunk, I have sown all my patrimony, which was

REV. SAMUEL KILPIN, 121 


considerable, I entered not into the ministry for a 
livelihood. I would not have turned my back to any 
man of business. I might have had an honorable, a 
lucrative trade, if I had chosen it when young, but my 
heart burned within me, and I gave my soul to God, 
and with all its energies I have labored in his vineyard. 

“T often tremble for the health of my dear wife; her 
looks bespeak illness, but few complainings escape 
her lips. May God preserve her life until she has sup- 
ported my dying head, and closed my eyes in death 
I feel pensive, but calm,” 


To a Friend. January 3, 1830.

which are discharging, are at present a sore trial; [ 
cannot walk across the room but in great agony. On 
Monday I shall be fifty-six years old. Pray for me. 
fam sorely oppressed with pain. O Lord, undertake 
for me. Pained as I am, I.had rather the affliction 
should be sanctified than removed; nor would I ex- 
change afflictions and mercy with any man, or Chris- 
tian, in the world. My soul delights itself in God, 
whose I am, and whom I serve.”

To Miss B———. 


“ My prarest Mary,—I am again the prisoner of the 
Lord, and, indeed, the trial is sore: but he has not ap- 
prehended me in anger, but for good; yet I find it re- 
quires more grace to be a waiting servant than a work-

Read that hymn, in Rippon’s collection. My brother 
and you will there see much of my feelings. Iam 
now very unwell, and I think, a few days ago, was 
much worse. My appetite, though bad, is very much 
better; yet I have much to bear, and need very much 
your prayers. Let me entreat them. Life is so uncer- 
tain, that I determined to send you a line once more 
in my own hand-writing. It may be the last; yet I 
do not, in my own mind, think it will. I have said, 
our dear Lord has made me his prisoner, but it is in 
love. He is all love, and I love him. O love him, and 
bless him for his love to me. He sought me, found me, 
and revealed his love to me very young—only four- 
teen when the thorough work of conversion to him 
was done. I then was able to receive him in the ful- 
ness of his salvation. Jesus complete, without good 
works, and with his grace against bad works. I have 
been now forty-one years in his service; and though I 
have oft forgot his loving-kindness, it faileth net, and 
I think Tlove him more than ever Idid. He is all my 
hope and joy. In him, in him alone, abstracted from 
all other considerations, I have a hope that changeth 
not. Ifhe is able, lam safe; and Iam quite willing to 
be nothing, if ever he becomes unable or unwilling to 
save a poor sinner: I have none beside him. 


‘Other refuge have I none,’ &c. 


All who fled for refuge of old, were safe until the

high priest died. So am I until Christ dies; but he

When I lost my dear Samuel, many times my heart

I fear. But, O! my dear Mary, if I die! No, no; if I 
do not go to heaven, I shall never go to hell. I hate 
the inhabitants, I hate sin; I hate sin in all its shapes. 
I love God for what he is—his character. I love him 
for his holiness: I love him in his dear Son. Tell all 
young people that it is religion that can give sweetest 
pleasure. O, my dear Mary, if I do get into the pulpit 
once more, my heart shall proclaim his dear love. I 
am really much better, though I am a total prisoner. 
I think my affliction is for more work, and I shall 
again see you in the flesh; yet I can speak but very

“ Tell Dr. R. how much [ envy him the pleasure of

saved by rich and sovereign grace. Halleluiah to God

work to die; but our dear Lord has stripped death of 
all its horrors, and made him a friend: see how they 
talked of it in old times, ‘Sleep,’ ‘slept,’ ‘ sleep.’ ”

A few weeks before his departure, the school chil- 


REV. SAMUEL “KILPIN. 127 


dren hail their annual treat of a cake, &c. They 
were arranged in the garden-walks, that their keloved 
friend mightsee them. Two hundred scholars enjoyed 
this treat, whilst their kind minister sat at his window, 
and remarked—“‘ With what pleasure have I fed these 
dear children for many years! and now, blessed be God, 
I can rejoice to see them fed by others.” His feelings 
here were overpowered, and he could scarcely sustain 
the view. Whilst they passed and bowed before him, 
his face shone with pleasure, and amidst the tears his 
eyes were directed heavenward. 

God also graciously granted him.a farewell sight 
of many dear relatives. Three brothers, two sisters, 
and four nieces saw him not many days before he 
fell asleep in Jesus. The closing scene was now ap- 
proaching. 

Nothing unconnected with Jesus and his church 
could long interest him. “Let the dead bury their 
dead,” he would say, “my time is short and precious. ” 

During nearly forty years of his public ministry he 
had not what he termed, on the average, one silent 
Sabbath in the year. During the ten years preceding 
his illness, he preached equal to a sermon on each day. 
Perhaps at no period of his life were praises and 
thanksgivings more frequently offered up to his God. 
His illness continued two years and a half, which pe- 

_viod was decidedly the most useful and honored of 
God, in the conversion of souls, of any in his life. 

When kept at home on the Sabbath, he endeavored 
to meet the worshippers in the house of God in spirit, 
and alternately prayed and sung aloud, substituting 
reading for preaching. Before the worship was regu- 
lar in the Garden Chapel, he was often there alone, 
and occupied the pulpit, whilst his family were in South-

street. On one occasion a niece entered unperceived,

“Take my poor heart just as itis, 
“Set up therein thy throne; 

So shall I love thee above all, 
“ And live to thee alone.” 


He then said aloud, “I love the habitation of thy house, 
and the place where thine honor dwelleth.” “Blessed 
are they who dwell in thine house; they shall be still

On observing his niece, he remarked-—“‘ What a mer-

him. To be here does me good. I have met with the 
Master of assemblies, though not the assembly.” 

Thus his great personal afflictions were beguiled and 
sanctified. Thus he glorified God in the fire. Amidst 
the night watches, when he thought himself unheard,

Not long before his death he suddenly became blind. 
His own words will best express his feelings. 


“On rising from my chair, I found that I could not 
see an object. I was about to exclaim, alas! alas! 
when that blessed word, as by a soft still voice, reached 
my heart,—‘Fear not, I am with thee; be not dis- 
mayed, Iam thy God; I will help thee.’ In a moment 
I became still as a child, and said, I can proclaim the 
honor of thy name without sight; the Holy Ghost 
will not reject a blind servant, but give him especial 
assistance.” 


When in the prospect of total blindness, he remark- 
ed to a friend—“ Were you to inform me that I should 
die in half an hour, I would shake you by the hand, 
and thank you; but I should be content to live a thou- 
sand years for the good of souls.” 

On no other ground did he desire his life to be pro- 
longed. 

His sight was restored in a short time, but his 
strength continued to fail; his mind, however, was 
supported. He wrote to a friend, about five weeks be- 
fore his decease, as follows: 


“YT have been supported thus far in my affliction, 
and got through the thickest of the storm and tempest, 
but with the vessel deeply laden always, and, at times, 
the tackling of the ship on the full stretch. Often 
some part of it gives way, but the ship is secure, though 
on her beam ends, or pitching dreadfully. ‘Why are 
you not afraid? said a hardy sailor to a child in a 
storm. ‘My father is at the helm,’ said the boy. 
There is a good deal in that, especially when sun, 


30 LIFE OF THE 


moon, and stars appear not. I am going out again to 
sea, for how long I cannot tell, but there are symptoms 
of another attack. I cannot walk a step; truly Iam 
a prisoner of the Lord. When he gives the word I 
am set at liberty under heaven, or taken to it. I know 
whom I have trusted, and that he is able to keep what 
was committed to him forty years ago. If he is not 
able to save, it is of no consequence to be annihilated ; 
as to his being willing, that is quite clear. ‘He came 
to seek and to save that which was lost’—absolutely 
lost. Through grace, faith lays hold of him as he is. 
There are many condemnations against sinners, but 
now, in Christ Jesus, there are none that can stand. 
God, who is justice, is just, and the justifier of all them 
that believe. As much a poor publican as Abraham— 
there is no difference. Here is my support. 


“Other refuge have I none.” 


These particulars of the last hours of Mr. Kilpin 
may, to some, appear to be unnecessarily protracted ; but 
the Savior has many whom he loves that are sick. 
such may, from these details, gather support under 
their own sufferings in the furnace. 

About a fortnight before he left this world, while at 
breakfast, he remarked—“ The past night I have felt 
willing to die. I said, in a wakeful hour, ‘Now, Lord, 
lettest thou thy servant depart in peace; my eyes have 
seen thy salvation.’” At another time, “I could al- 
ways say during my illness, that to live was Christ, 
and to die gain; but I could not say until now, that it 
was better to depart.” Again, on waking from a doze 
“T thought I was gliding into eternity, and that it was 
sweet thus to leave the world.” He appeared as though 
he felt disappointed to return to this world. “I am 


REY. SAMUEL KILPIN. 131 


willing to die at any hour” was very often repeated. 
On arranging some papers, he remarked—“ It was said 
t> Hezekiah, ‘Set thy house in order, for thou shalt 
die, and not live;’” but added, “recollect, he lived 
fifteen years after this period.” 

{lis little church was the last object of his solicitude 
on earth; the last link broken. When he was able tc 
say, “I can now leave the dear little church calmly 
with my dear Lord, its great Head ; it is his church, he 
purchased it with his precious blood; he loves it more 
than I can love it, it cost him dear ;” he felt as though 
he should never recover. The last object of his prayers 
was answered. 

At different times during his confinement he burned 
all the letters and papers he could collect. The frag- 
nients that remained were in books, and a few escaped 
by being pressed into a desk. Not one letter, except 
on business, was rescued ; nor had he an idea that one 
existed. This plan was adopted, it is supposed, from 
an idea that his wife would die before him, which was 
often thought probable, and that he might then be un- 
able to arrange his papers. A diary of ten years was 
destroyed: this he afterward regretted. 

The power of God was made perfect in weakness. 
He would never admit for a moment that his dear Lord 
was chastening him in anger. The Lord’s day, three 
weeks prior to his death, he preached in much weak- 
ness, but his aim was to preach usefully. This had 
ever been his highest ambition, how to win souls to 
Christ. Baxter’s motto was his—‘“I will preach as 
though ! never should preach again: I will preach as 
a dying man to dying men.” On the last Sabbath he 
spent upon earth, he particularly desired to see every 
member of his little church. He addressed each; many

prayers were offered ; but it did not appear that he him- 
self, or they, considered this as the parting scene. On 
Monday, every preparation was made for a winter’s 
residence, by day and night, in his parlor study. At 
ten o’clock he complained of being very unwell, and 
requested that some portions of Scripture should be 
repeated, the words of his dear Lord, on which he had 
fed and feasted during his illness. On Tuesday his 
cough was troublesome, and he remarked that he was 
more likely to die from consumption than dropsy. He 
enjoyed a peaceful night, more free from pain and 
restlessness than usual. On Wednesday he rose at six 
o’clock, his accustomed hour, and was drawn to the 
breakfast room: a general drowsiness followed. His 
kind and skilful medical attendant gave his opinion 
that death would speedily approach. A kind friend 
sent him a partridge. He sharpened his Enife and 
carved the bird himself, and partook of a small part. 
He was then gently informed of Dr. - *s opinion, 
and appeared surprised, saying he had no idea from 
what symptoms the conclusion was drawn; but calmly 
added, “ Well, you know it makes no difference, all is 
settled.” Drowsiness increased, and he consented to 
be assisted to his bed. A restless night followed, with 
some wanderings. On Thursday morning he requested 
his station family to assemble. This included the or- 
phans and matron. They surrounded his bed, when 
he fully explained the nature and effects of his faith in 
Christ, and wished it to be distinctly understood that 
Jesus Christ was alone the fuundation of his hope. 
This he said was his dying testimony. Christ was all 
his hope and joy, his blood and righteousness his only 
plea. He was enabled to receive the Savior with his 
whole heart, for,a whole and complete salvation; and

which were his joy in dying moments. “Though af-

On Friday morning he evidently could still distin- 
guish his wife’s voice, and returned an affectionate 
pressure with his dying hand to some questions asked, 
and he endeavored to raise his dying lips for the last 
parting expression of endearment. Soon his senses 
failed, and he lay calmly until half-past seven in the 
evening, when in a moment the approach of death was 
visible, and, without a struggle or a groan, he slept in 
Jesus, September 17th, 1830. 


The reader has now been presented with a brief re- 
cord of the life of a most indefatigable servant of God ; 
a faithful minister of Christ, whose meat and drink 
was to do his Master’s work. 

The particulars have been related with simplicity, 
for they are mostly recorded by his own pen; they are 
the unpremeditated effusions of his mind, and he was 
well known to all his friends as “an Israelite indeed, 
in whom there was no guile”—the only guile ever to 
be found in him was, that he might win souls to Christ, 
Tn these endeavors he was ever willing to become all 
Pings to all men, yet never departing from the simpli- 
city and truth of the Gospel. 

In person Mr. Kilpin was rather above the middle 
s'ze_ and inclined to corpulency, which increased in 


REV. SAMUEL KILPI®. 135 


his latter years. His sedentary life, after he took up 
his residence in St. Sidwells, promoted this. The va- 
ried occupations concentrated on that spot, though 
most active and laborious for the mind, yet did not 
call for bodily exercise in proportion. The urbanity co 
his manners made him acceptable to all ranks, though 
he never mingled in general society unless duty called 
him. In temper he was exceedingly mild, tender, and 
affectionate ; the manner in which he won the hearts 
of children, and engaged their attention, cannot be 
adequately described: he was always at home with 
them. The intense enjoyment he derived from their 
society showed how deeply painful those bereavements 
must have been Ly which he was written childless, 
His labors among the young may be estimated from 
the fact, that for many years scarcely ever less than 
five hundred chiluven engaged his attention in each 
successive week. He was an instance of most acute 
sensibility beneath somewhat of a sturdy exterior. At 
times he was too sensitive of real or supposed unkind- 
ness for his own peace; but he never harbored resent- 
ment, and if “old Adam,” as he used to express him- 
self, arose for a moment, he was speedily subdued by 
the divine grace which dwelt within the servant of 
Christ. Nor was it often that pain was designedly 
given him. But many did not understand his excel- 
lences, and some others would not make allowances 
for those differences which are so often manifest in the 
diversity of the gifts of the Holy Spirit. We do not 
sufficiently remember that “there are differences of 
administrations, but the same Lord; and there are di- 
versities of operations, but it is the same God whick 
worketh all in all. But the manifestation of the Spir ' 
is given to every man to profit withal.” 


136 LIFE OF THE 


His intenseness of feeling, however, was often a sore 
trial to him, and at times caused some to mistake him; 
but it led him to walk humbly with his God. He was 
often driven to his refuge by a breath which a sterner 
mind would scarcely have felt. All this made him 
live closer to his Savior, and rendered him more anx- 
ious to share the sorrows and alleviate the sufferings 
of others. : 

Asa preacher, Mr, Kilpin had many souls given him 
for his hire. The points he pressed upon the people, 
though studied and prepared beforehand, were urged 
from the heart. He would often seize upon a promi- 
nent circumstance, or topic, that arose from his sub- - 
ject, and press it home with a force and power which 
few could hear unmoved. 

The reader has already seen that a considerable por- 
tion of his preparation for the pulpit was earnest 
prayer. Every action of his life, it may be said, was 
begun, carried on, and ended in prayer. 

His literary acquirements were very respectable, but 
he made no pretensions to superior attainments, and 
never desired to be accounted as having knowledge or 
learning beyond what he really possessed. 

In all points of business Mr. Kilpin was very cor- 
rect, and fully justified the statement in one of his last 
letters, that “he would not have turned his back to 
any man of business.” Friends who witnessed his 
qualifications in this respect, bear full testimony to his 
abilities in such affairs. His habits of business and 
precision in all pecuniary matters enabled him to effect 
much with very small means. When his accounts 
were enlarged, by adding the orphan asylum to his 
other objects of charity, they were kept with equal pre- 
cision, though his health was declining. The account- 


REV. SAMUEL KILPiN. 137 


900k was headed with a motto, which should be adopted 
ay every follower of Christ, especially those connected 
with pecuniary trusts. It was, “ Owe no man any 
thing but love.” These accounts were kept in the 
clearest manner. The particulars were clearly stated, 
and the result might be seen at a glance. ‘Those who 
have been much connected with public institutions, 
well know that painful circumstances would often be 
avoided, if this plan, so expressly in accordance with 
the precepts of the Gospel, were adopted by all who 
dedicate their time and talents to promote works and 
labors of love. 

Mr. Kilpin sought not popularity, in the common 
acceptation of the term; from this snare he was de- 
livered; but he possessed the hearts of his people and 
his friends to an unusual degree. By them he was be- 
loved. The simplicity of his manner, and the lively 
way in which he narrated facts, with his powerful ap- 
peals both to the conscience and tke affections, en: 
abled him often to hold the attention of extensive au- 
ditories, and influenced them powerfully. Hence his 
assistance was frequently sought on public occasions, 
and he readily aided in every good work, when able so 
to do. Distinction of parties and shades of opinion 
he cared little for; though he never would compromise 
his own views of the truth, either as to doctrine or d s- 
cipline. A friend who knew him intimately, and cor- 
responded with him regularly, during twelve years, in 
terms of the greatest freedom, discussing both doctrinal 
and practical subjects, states, that during the whole pe 
riod Mr. K. never once urged, or even recommende: 
to him, the peculiar views which he himself maintain-. 
ed in reference to certain points of Christian practice, 


though they occasionally came under consideration. 
12* 


138 LIFE OF THE 


There are many, doubtless, of various persuasions, who 
will think Mr. Kilpin wrong in this respect; but he 
knew his friend to be as firm in his views as he him- 
self was in his own; he believed him to be a follow- 
er of Christ, and he felt that there was common ground 
on which they could act, far more than sufficient to 
employ all the powers they could bring into unison, 
and he felt it would be wrong that time should be lost, 
and harmony endangered, by entering upon other sub- 
jects. Often did he deplore the pertinacity of many 
professors upon the more minute, yet not unimportant 
points, in which they differed with their brethren; 
and though ever ready to oppose error from without, 
he was reluctant to stir up hostilities within the church 
of Christ. “Let all your things be done with charity,” 
was a sentiment he ever desired to urge. 

Mr. Kilpin’s various plans of useful benevolence 
made him increasingly known in his latter years and 
wherever known he was respected and esteemed. He 
was considered by some as eccentric, and blamed 
by others as “enthusiastic ; ”but slander had no ground 
on which to attack his moral worth. Some of the ser- 
vants of God have had their usefulness lessened by er 
rors in conduct; others have had things laid to their 
charge which they knew not, while they have found 
it difficult to free themselves from the imputations 
falsely cast upon them. These trials Mr. Kilpin was 
spared; he went forward in his course unimpeded by 
any such painful circumstances. He felt this to be 
a mercy, and frequently expressed his gratitude for it, 
he knew that his usefulness was much aided there- 
by, and his earnest desire was not to be accounted a 
great man, but a useful man. This literally was his 
wish, and it was granted. Whatever differences of 


REV. SAMUEL KILPIN. 139 


opinion may exist as to his plans and proceedings, all 
admit that the results showed that he was a useful 
man. He seemed to realize the counsel of the Chris- 
tian poet: 


“ Be useful where thou livest; that they may 

“ Both want and wish thy pleasing presence still. 
“Kindness, good parts, great duties, are the way 
“To compass this. Find out men’s wants and will, 
“And meet them there. All worldly joys go less 
“To the one joy of dc.ng kindnesses.” Herbert. 


And let it be remembered, that though his kindness 
to the bodies of men abounded, his kindnesses for their 
souls were still more abundant. He was anxious to 
comfort those who were in any trouble, by the com- 
fort wherewith he was himself comforted of God.

SOME PARTICULARS

SAMUEL WYKE KILPIN. 


WRITTEN BY HIS FATHER, 


The Rey. S. Kilpin, having lost an only and beloved son at 
the age of thirteen, under circumstances peculiarly trying 
to his parental feelings, endeavored to commit to paper some 
particulars respecting his child, but was obliged repeated- 
ly to lay down his pen; and, though he resumed the task, he 
was unable to complete it as he intended. Nor did he makea 
correction, or even read the whole of what he had written, al- 
though he desired and fully purposed that it should eventually 
be published. The following pages contain the substance of 
the Memoir, being somewhat abridged from the u.iinal, which 
is published in a separate form. It is here added, as the narti- 
eulars related, and the remarks which accompany them, au 
much to elucidate the character and history of the parent. 


The birth of Samuet Wyxe Kier was particularly 
interesting to his parents, because their three elder 
children had been previously removed by death. His 
infant days were healthful and sprightly. When rea- 
son began to dawn, which was remarkably early, it 
was his parents’ delightful employ to direct and regu- 
late its daily increase. His temper was mild, his man- 
ners gentle, yet strong passions would often fire his 
infant soul. This led to fervent prayer, that He who 
had communicated such passions and faculties would 
he pleased to give them a right direction, would sanc- 
tify his infant mind, and lead it to Himself. 

That Divine Being who hears the parent’s prayer, 
was most graciously pleased to give an early bias of 


142 LIFE OP 


piety to his infant mind. His tongue soon lisped the 
adorable name of his Creator and Redeemer, his little 
hands and eyes were early directed heavenward, and 
with his feeble voice he spake in prayer to that God 
who hears the first breathings of the soul after himself. 
Before he could articulate distinctly, which was at ar 
early period, he had learned to write At three years 
old he could read his Bible as distinct and with as 
wiuch propriety as at any future period cf his life. 

I felt that the Lord had lent him to me, and never 
wished an interest in him but as a ioan from my heaven- 
ly Father. Such were the pleasing traits in his dispos. 
tion, from day to day, that a pious neighbor often re 
marked, “I believe that child was sanctified from his 
birth.” Many thought with her, and perhaps I should 
have judged the same of any other child but “my own.” 

“ His breath was in his nostrils ;” of this I was feel- 
ingly reminded by several attacks of croup. His mo- 
ther’s life appeared to be bound up in the child; I sus- 
pected with fearful awe a species of idolatry in her, 
from which probably my own mind was not free; for, 
alas! how visible is that dreadfully deluding sin in the 
conduct of many parents! how well timed, but little 
heeded, is the impressive caution of Dr. Watts, ‘‘ Be- 
ware of blooming idols in white frocks!” I dreaded 
lest we should indulge in that ensnaring, frequently 
unsuspected, hidden sin, which imperceptibly entwines 
around the parental heart, and produces the most dan- 
gerous effects on the soul. Necessary is it to watch 
an excess of our best feelings. O ye affectionate pa- 
rents, watch and pray against this easily besetting sin, 
which, if not speedily checked, will, ere you are aware, 
Le as a root of bitterness springing up to trouble you! 
The sight of this dangerous rock often alarmed me, 
and led me frequently to weeping and supplication at 
the footstool of mercy; and, on a faithful examination 
of my heart, I can adore Him who made the dear child 
as a stepping-stone to my soul in its ascent to His 
strone; and, when clasping him in my arms with un- 
utterable delight, I generally felt my heart adoring his 


SAM™JEL WYKE KILPIN. 148 


Father and my Father, his Savior and my Savior. ” 
From his fourth year his mind was extremely inqui- 
sitive, his attention was often riveted by religious con- 
versation, and his questions were of a nature which 
dues not admit of speedy or satisfactory answers. I 
will mention a few questions asked, and the remarks 
made by him, between the fourth and sixth years of 
his age. 

We had during this period generally six students as 
‘nmates in our family; the dear child frequently re- 
zollected the time for delivering lectures, and, quietly 
entering the room, took a station where he could see 
and hear all that passed; at times I permitted him to 
hang on my neck, where his every look, every gesture, 
manifested his close attention, and often reproved the 
listlessness of his seniors. 

We had a large garden; I occasionally amused my- 
self in its culture. His spirits, gay as a lark, made him 
expert in all boyish exercises. To collect weeds or 
stones in a little barrow was to him enjoyment, and no 
one was more alert in upsetting the loads into a pit 
made for their reception. 

Whilst working in the garden, I heard his dear voice 
(methinks I hear it now) exclaiming, “ Papa, papa! I 
want to ask you a question. Where was my soul be- 
fore it came into this body?” “ My dear boy,” I replied, 
“it would be better for you to inquire where it will go, 
if it were now to leave the body?” “QO, papa!” he 
said, with an inexpressible look, “it would go to hea- 
ven; that is sure, that is settled.” “Alas! my dear boy, 
I wish it may be so settled,” I rejoined; “that would 
be of much greater importance than to know where it 
was before it entered into your body. What led you 
to propose such a question?” “J don’t know,” the 
dear fellow replied, “but it just entered my mind.” 
Then off he ran to his barrow. 

One of the students remarked to me, in his hearing, 
‘I should not wonder, sir, if Samuel were to become 

minister.” He caught the sentence, and with his 
usual energy exclaimed, “ A pretty minister, indeed, I 


144 LIFE OF 


should make, and cannot pray!” He repeated the sen- 
tence with increased eagerness, “ A pretty minister I 
should make,” and strongly accented the latter part, 
“T cannot pray!” “ What!” said I, with emotion, 
“cannot you pray?” ‘“ No,” he replied, “ not. not—” 
and immediately retreated. I perceived by the flush 
of his eye that something else was behind; but he dis- 
appeared, and I thought it right not to follow him. 

He was now past six years old, and his looks, his 
manners, and his general conversation, had excited ‘n 
‘ my soul the strongest anxieties for his eternal welfare. 
Lovely and amiable as he was, I felt assured from the 
sacred Scriptures, and from knowledge of the innate 
depravity of the human heart, that the renovation of 
soul, so emphatically called being “ created anew in 
Christ Jesus,” must be experienced by him prior to a 
meetness for heaven. 

_ As he approached his seventh year, my solicitude in- 
creased; with strong parental cries and tears I besieged 
the throne of God, in the name of my gracious Re- 
deemer, the friend of little ones. I entreated with 
Abraham’s God, with Abraham’s faith, that my Isaac 
might live to be the Lord’s. I sought that the life- 
giving principle might be communicated from on higk 
to his soul. He had talked about religion from the 
period that he first uttered a sentence. If any ques- 
tion was named to him on that subject, his answers 
always produced delight; yet I felt that no scriptural 
evidence could be drawn from a child in such peculiar 
circumstances, merely from sensible replies to ques- 
tions, and attention to the externals of religion. I knew 
the power of habit; I was assured of strong imitative 
dispositions in children, particularly those whose na- 
tural tempers.are excellent, and consequently disposed 
to be pleased and to communicate pleasure, which was 
most remarkably the case with my dear child. He 
possessed sufficient affection and discernment, easily 
to discover the sensations of delight which were ex- 
cited in his beloved mother’s bosom and mine, by these 
expressions of religion so sweetly uttered. Our joy 


SAMUEL WYKE KILPIN. 145 


was to him a high stimulus to further converse on the 
delightful theme, and his little heart was not insensible 
to praise, to parental praise! 

I felt conscious that his situation necessarily placed 
him in the way of seeing much of the forms of religion. 
Pions‘conversation, prayer, singing of hymns, constant 
attendance on the house of God, constituted a large 
share of the moral and religious atmosphere in which 
he lived; therefore I exceedingly dreaded lest his re- 
ligion should be merely learned—just the effect of edu- 
tation, and nothing more. 

These strong excitements of parental solicitude con- 
tinued long ; during that season I preached to my con- 
gregation from Psalm 126 : 6—“ He that goeth forth 
weeping, bearing precious seed, shall doubtless come 
again with rejoicing, bringing his sheaves with him.” 
My dear child was present. There was nothing more 
than usual in the application of the discourse, nor do 
I recollect, although his eye was generally fixed on 
me, that I once thought of him during the sermon. 
On returning to the house, being occupied in conver- 
sation, I noticed him less than usual, but found him 
resuming his usual place at my side, with his arm 
around my neck. He suddenly interrupted the con- 
versation, which rudeness was to him altogether un- 
common, for it was his practice to wait until the sub- 
ject was concluded before he ventured a question or 
remark. I was grieved and surprised to hear him say 
eagerly, ‘Papa, papa, you have been saying some 
things to-night which I do not exactly understand.” 
I calmly replied, “Should I do so again you must not 
wonder, I am older than you.” Ina tone [ never heard 
from him before or since, he said, “ I want no fun.” 
The manner, the expression, was entirely new; during 
his life he never uttered a word to either of his parents, ; 
till then, which could be deemed disrespectful. I felt + 
hurt, but made no reply. Dear creature, it was not 
disrespect, or want of affection, which induced his re- 
mark; but the hurry, the tumult, occasioned by a new 
chain of feelings he was not before conscious of. He 

‘ K 13 Kilpin. 


146 LIFE OF 


gently left my shoulder and my arm, and made a sud- 
den retreat from the room, when, with his heart all 
emotion and big with sorrow, he shut himself into a 
dark parlor, unperceived by any of the family. His 
mother accidentally went into the room some time 
after with a candle, and found the young penitent on 
his knees in earnest prayer. She was alarmed, and dis- 
turbed him: he arose, covered with confusion at being 
caught in that position, and effected an instant retreat 
to hisown room. Like Saul of Tarsus, he had often 
presented the service of the lip to his Maker; he had 
been taught with his first lisping voice to address his 
Savior, and I believe the child-like forms were never 
omitted morning or evening. 

O! how often had I entreated my God, that whilst 
my child was on his knees repeating the words of 
prayer, he would be pleased to breathe into his soul 
the spirit of prayer! My language was, O that the 
Lord the Spirit would make this child, like Samuel, a 
spiritual worshipper! He had feared the Lord from 
his infancy ; but now behold the lad at seven years old, 
whose heart the Lord opened, prostrate as a poor sin- 
ner at the foot of the cross, pleading for mercy through 
a crucified Savior. His mother had permitted him to 
enter his room alone, but kept her station near the 
door; his groans and sighs increased her alarm; she 
sent for me; I hastened to him, and witnessed a sight 
which can never be forgotten. Yes! I beheld my pe- 
titions answered in the agonizing prayers of my only 
child. With hands clasped together he sat on his bed, 
his eyes turned heavenward ; tears plentifully bedewed 
his cheeks. I heard him with indescribable anguish 
imploring the mercy of our Lord Jesus Christ. His 
affectionate mother, at a little distance from him, sat 
weeping. Convulsed with different feelings, I ap- 
proached his bed, when, taking my hand, he exclaimed 
in agony, “O, papa, papa! your sermon to-night has 
shown me that lam wrong, that I am a lost sinner. 
You said that those persons who had never gone forth 
weeping, would never return rejoicing ; and, alas! till 


SAMUEL WYKE KILPIN. 147 


now I never knew what sorrow for sin meant; there- 
fore I have no scriptural right to peace or joy : O, papa, 
papa! what shall I do?” 

“Be calm, my dear,” I replied, “ the case is not a lost 
one.” “But Iam lost,” he quickly replied. “ Yes, you 
are lost, but in the same way as a man or child who 
falls into a pit or river; should a friend pass at the mo- 
ment, and lift him from his perilous situation, he would 
be saved. So our Lord Jesus Christ stands ready and 
anxious to assist you, and bids you lock to him and be 
saved. You are made sensible that you need a Savior ; 
he waits to be gracious; he attends to the first breath 
of prayer from a penitent child; he loves the first 
groans or sighs sent from the heart. Look to him, my 
dear boy; accept of his mercy; pray to him; he will 
pardon and love you.” 

He again clasped his hands, and with a heart-rending 
groan said, “But I nave no right to joy, if destitute of 
real sorrow for sin.” 

“ But do you not feel your sins to be a burden?” 

“ Yes, now I do.” “Well then the Savior says 
‘Come unto me, all ye that are weary and heavy laden, 
and I will give you rest; look unto me, and be ye 
saved.” ” : 

He became more calm, but my feelings being com- 
pletely overpowered, obliged me to leave the room. 
When more composed, I returned, and found him in 
bitter agony, conversing with his mother, and again 
exclaiming, “Oh! I have been deceived all these 
years; what an awful thing is self-deception! O that 

_the Spirit would witness with my spirit that Iam born 
of God.” 

His mother was directing him to the Savior. He 
continued to pray earnestly until, exhausted, he fell 
into the arms of sleep. She watched by him until 
midnight, rejoicing and weeping. 

TE then felt in an especial manner the excellence of 
the Christian religion; to what a sure and solid foun- 
dation of hope could I direct the bleeding heart of my 
dear child! O thou “ Lamb of God, who takest away 


148 LIFE O} 


the sin of the world,” the potency of thy sacrifice, the 
advantages derived from thy atoning blood, thy all- 
powerful intercession, were sought for and granted. 
‘To what other source of comfort could I have directed 
my agonized child 2? miserable comforters would all 
other refuges have been, altogether insufficient to cover 
from the storm, to shield from the tempest, the terrified 
bosom of an awakened sinner. Nothing but thy blood, 
O Jesus, can do helpless sinners good. I committed 
my weeping son in prayer to the care of Him who 
graciously styles himself the “God of love,” the 
“ Friend of sinners,’ who alone can communicate 
peace to the troubled conscience. Language cannot 
describe what I felt on entering my closet; the leading 
expression at such a season, when an answer to many 
years of fervent prayer had been received, was, “Ac- 
cept, O Lord, the thanks I cannot utter; the praises my 
heart feels, but cannot express; my soul is lost in won- 
der, love, and praise.” 

My mind was deeply humbled at being thus made 
the spiritual father of my dear and only child; my 
mind became calm, and I felt a delightful satisfaction 
and trust for the future; I gave him wholly to the 
Lord, and said, “ Do with him whatsoever seemeth to 
thee good ;” and whilst he was entwined around my 
heart with firm parental and Christian bonds, my soul 
involuntarily arose in holy adoration to his God and 
my God. 

[had not sat long the next morning in my study 
thus exercised, when J heard his dear little footsteps 
near my door. He entered with his usual bow, and 
salutation, “ Good morning, papa.” “Good morning, 
my darling; how are you? Come to my arms, I want 
to ask you a question or two. When at your door last 
night, Iheard you exclaim with great eagerness, ‘ O 
that the Spirit would witness with my spirit that I am 
a child of God” Did you mean that the great and 
blessed God would speak to you, a poor little sinner, 
with a voice from heaven ?” 

With a look that now rends my heart, he said, “No 
fapa; I should be ashamed of such a thought.” 


SAMUEL WYKE KILPIN. 149 


“Well, my child,” I replied, “I am glad that you 
had no such desire or expectation. But tell me what 
you did mean ?” 

“T meant,” he said, with the firmness of a man fifty 
years old, “ that I wished the Holy Spirit would work 
in my heart what he has written in the Bible, and then 
let me see it.” 

“ And do you now desire that your whole heart may 
be sanctified and devoted to God ?” 

“ Yes, papa,” he replied with strong emotion, “ I 
ao.” 

“ Cleave then to the Savior, my dear child, praying 
for his mercy, and it shall be granted.” 

From that period it might be said that he was a 
regularly praying child, with the exception of some 
few neglects, over which he wept and mourned; but 
those seasons never continued longer than a day or 
two. 

A new principle now pervaded his actions. Before 
he felt the comforts of pardoning grace, he trembled 
in view of God’s justice; now his soul knew the joys 
of forgiveness, he felt the main-spring of all his endea- 
vors to be the ennobling principle of divine love. From 
the most close investigation of his character, I had 
every reason to believe that his deep and often return- 
ing sorrow for sin produced the “ peaceable fruits of 
righteousness.” “ Holiness to the Lord” had been 
written on his heart by the Holy Ghost, consequently 
the fruits were holy.. The warfare with in-dwelling 
sin had commenced, and terminated only at death; he 
had, though young, to conflict, “as we do now, with 
‘sins, and doubts, and fears ;” and his mind, although 
renewed, would sometimes struggle to oppose the will 
of God. This drove him to a throne of grace for 
fresh strength to combat the world, the flesh, and the 
devil. The Lord gave him quick admission to glory; 
but, whilst an inhabitant of earth, most largely endowed 
him with his holy sanctifying influences. Every day 
proved to my judgment that real conversion of soul to 
God had been communicated from on high. ee a 

13* 


150 LIFE OF 


experienced that great change of heart termed, by the 
Redeemer, being “ born again,” and destitute of which 
all other changes would have been ineffectual to his 
salvation. Without this soul-transforming change all 
his amiable qualities would have vanished at death, 
and he would have been now the companion of differ- 
ent beings from those with whom he associated on 
earth ;—tremendous thought! Parents, be not satisfied 
with superficial religion for your children. Be not 
hasty to pronounce their state safe on slight evidences ; 
but wrestle mightily with God till “Christ be formed in 
them, the hope of glory.” 

I have before remarked his proficiency in reading 
and writing, of both which employments he was ex- 
ceedingly fond. His memory was strong and particu- 
larly tenacious. It seemed firmly to retain what was 
once committed to it by reading, hearing, and obser- 
vation. At five years old he was sufficiently acquainted 
with the construction of the English and Latin gram- 
mars to justify his learning to write Greek letters, which 
he did with great neatness. At about that age he could 
repeat the Lord’s Prayer in Latin and Greek, with an 
accent and correctness which would have reflected 

\credit on a student of eighteen. Here I would again 
remind my readers that the great God had designed 
for him a short life only, yet intended that, abridged 
as it was, it should not be devoid of usefulness. His 
mind appeared to pass through the different stages of 
infancy, childhood, and manhood, in all their various 
gradations and beautiful variety. In the course of one 
day I have heard him converse with all the wisdom of 
a sage on various topics introduced by himself, with 
astonishing ease; the next hour I have seen him, with 
the agility of a squirrel, spring upon the bough of a 
tree which he had chosen as a seat whilst studying his 
Greek grammar ; then, swift as a roe, follow a kite, or 
cause his hoop to roll swiftly along the walks, or bound 
over the new raised garden ground. His animal spirits 
moved light as air; his piety was of the most cheerful 
cast; he learned from his beloved Bible that the great 


SAMUEL WYKE KILPIN. 151 


God, although glorious in holiness, was a God of love, 
who delighted to make his children happy, and to see 
them so; and he found that religion’s “ ways are ways 
of pleasantness, and all her paths peace.” 

The visitation of mercy to my dear child had pro- 
duced a great and beneficial effect among his relatives 
and pious neighbors; but his fond parents still pondered 
them in their hearts. On the part of his mother, it 
increased to a much lamented absorption of soul; I 
believe she was never happy except he was in her sight. 
‘When absent from him for a short time, she beguiled 
the tedious moments by having something belonging 
to him in her hand, or else in preparing something for 
his return. The delight with which she daily wit- 
nessed his opening mind—for it expanded with aston- 
ishing rapidity—was often completely overpowered by 
fearful anxieties respecting him. Her mind was agi- 
tated occasionally by excess of joy, and at other times 
by dreadful forebodings. From this period my own 
anxieties, on various accounts, were multiplied, but 
they led me daily to surrender him to the Lord. With 
much care and labor [ endeavored to direct his mind, 
and it appeared ever ready for any pursuit; his whole 
conduct was humble, tractable, and mild. 

That all-wise Providence who directs our every 
step, removed us from the place of his nativity to Ex- 
eter; here he had a larger field of excitement and 
exertion; and as he grew in stature rapidly, his faculties 
proportionably enlarged ; and while he grew in stature, 
he grew in favor with God and man. After the busi- 
ness of the day, he would frequently visit the habita- 
tions of the poor, and read and pray with them. I was 
fearful lest their caresses should make him vain; but 
his soul loathed itself, and I trust he walked humbly 
with his God. Hestudied hard; the history of his own 
country was very familiar to him; and as I never took 
a journey of any length without him, I was frequently 
delighted with his remarks on places, or his inquiries 
after historical subjects, as we pursued our rides. On 
one of these interesting tours, to me more interesting 


152 LIFE OF 

now than at that period, the sun shone sweetly upon 
us, the heavens, clear and calm, were emblematic of 
the peace we both enjoyed, whilst the hills and the 
dales silently admonished us that life was a checquered 
scene. 

I had many serious thoughts concerning his future 
life, and secretly determined, if it should be the will 
of God, that he should regularly study medicine and 
divinity ; but the idea of any one entering into the 
ministry, except absolutely called of God, is to me so 
awful, that [had much rather have seen him carrying 
John Bunyan’s basket than enter into his pulpit, if 
not called of God as my townsman John Bunyan was. 
I thought, therefore, now was the proper period of 
knowing his mind. I said, “ My darling, tell me what 
profession or employment you think you should pre- 
fer in future life?” 

Hesitating a little, but it was the hesitation of full- 
ness, he replied, “There is no employment equal to 
that of a minister of the Gospel; but a man ought to 
know that God calls him to that work.” 

I replied, on purpose to try him, “So every man 
ought to have the leadings of Providence manifested 
before he engages in any employment.” 

‘He answered: “ Yes, papa; but if a man makes a 
mistake in any other profession, it is only a mistake 
to himself; but if he enters into the ministry, it is 
more of God: any other profession is worldly, this is 
spiritual.” 

“Then you think a person ought to be more assured 
that it is God’s will, as it respects the ministry, than in 
any thing else?” “ Certainly,” he replied. 

* But, supposing the will of God is ascertained, then 
how would your mind be disposed to the ministry ?” 

“ More than any thing!” This was spoken with 
great fervor of mind. It was a delightful tour to me, 
we had sweet conversation together. The fields, the 
meadows, the hills, the vales, all,conspired to help our 
meditation; even the hedges afforded amusement and 
instruction: he could name most of the herbs, shrubs, 


4 


SAMUEL WYKE KILPIN. 153 


and flowers, as we passed them, and relate their medi- 
einal qualities. Dr. Hill’s Jarge folio herbal had been 
a favorite book of reference. Conscientious objections 
on my own mind to some Latin classical authors, pre- 
vented me from directing his studies to them. To me 
it appears painful to teach any thing that would lead 
the mind afterward to wish it had never known; so 
that, in fact, all he learnt was introductory. Five times 
he passed through the Eton grammar, twice through 
the Westminster Greek grammar, and once through 
Baily’s Hebrew grammar. I translated and abridged 
part of ‘Celsus de Medicina,’ and we were just about 
commencing a regular reading of Poole’s Synopsis, 
when the enemy of man, and the friend of saints, made 
his appearance, and deranged all our earthly plans for 
better—yes, for better, “ordered in all things and sure.” 
The history of his own country, and its geography, 
were perfectly familiar to him; and natural history 
being a source of great delight, he made it matter of 
research. He frequently would intersperse his con- 
versation with quotations from the history of nations. 
Here also he was much at home. As he now grew 
muscular and strong for his age, we beguiled many 
hours in our garden, which was his favorite amuse- 
ment. Here the parent and the child were lost in 
cheerful familiarities. He had a keenness of repartee 
that amused, but was always within bounds; and if it 
verged on too great a familiarity, it was not his fault. 

He often amused himself at the piano, for which he 
had some taste ; but in singing he was an adept. With 
a chaste judgment and a voice not often equalled, he 


ted the tunes in public worship some few years, and 


such was its power, that each note received its full 
cadence, and the ear was charmed. Being myself fond 
of music from my childhood, we often joined in sing- 
ing when we walked the lonely lane or the secluded 
field, or traveled on the road. Many a mile did we, in 
sweet heart melody, sing the praises of him we adored, 
his God and my God. 

He was my companion, my confidant ; he witnessed 


154 LIFE OF 


my cares, my tears. He rejoiced and sorrowed with 
ine. L valued his judgment. Several circumstances 
had occasioned me great vexation, which I had ex- 

pressed in a letter to an individual from whom the 
' grievance sprung; my feelings were raised to strong 
expressions of resentment. He entered my study, ! 
handed him the unsealed letter, which he read?with 
great attention, and returned in silence. “ Well, my 
boy, what think you ?” 

“All that you have written, papa, is true, and he 
deserves much more; but if I were you, my dear papa, 
I would not send it to-day.” 

On the following day he inquired—“ Is the letter 
seat ?” 

“No,” was the reply. “I hope it will not be sent 
to-day,” he rejoined. 

On the next inquiry, he was told that it was burnt. 

““O, my dear papa,” he exclaimed, “how much more 
happy will you feel by permitting the flames to destroy 
your resentment, however just !” 

At another time, when I was in deep distress, and he 
stood weeping over me, conversing with me on the 
subject of affliction, he remarked—“ It is not the cir- 
cumstance of the affliction, but the spirit we feel under 
it, that gives the largest wound.” 

As we were in the street one evening, a number of 
rude persons appeared to be mocking, and repeating 
what we had said. I felt hurt at their manner. He 
instantly said—‘“ We are not sure that they intended 
any disrespect to us, and it is much more pleasant for 
us to believe that they did not. If they are rude, it 
may make them uncomfortable afterward ; but why 
should we punish ourselves by feeling hurt?” Ina day 
or two we found that their conversation and manner 
had no reference to u3. 

On one occasion when he had offended me, I deemed 
it right to manifest displeasure ; and when he asked a 
question about the business of the day, I was sho:t and 
reserved in my answers to him. An hour or more 
elapsed. The time was nearly arrived when he waa

SAMUEL WYKE KILPIN. 155) 


to repeat his lessons. He came into my study, and 
said—“ Papa, I cannot learn my lessons except you 
are reconciled. Iam very sorry I have offended you; 
I hope you will forgive me: I think I shall never offend 
again.” 

I replied, “ All I want is to make you sensible of 
your fault ; when you acknowledge it, you know all is 
easily reconciled with me.” 

“Then, papa,” said he, “give me the token of 
reconciliation, and seal it with a kiss.” The hand was 
given, and the seal most heartily exchanged on each 
side. 

“Now,” exclaimed the dear boy, “I will learn 
Latin and Greek with any body,” and was hastening to 
his study. 

“Stop, stop,” I called after him, “have you not a 
heavenly Father? If what you have done has been 
evil, he is displeased, and you must apply to him for 
forgiveness.” 

With tears starting in his eyes, he said, “ Papa, I 
went to him first: I knew that except he was recon- 
ciled I could do nothing,” and with tears fast rolling 
down his cheek he added, “I hope, I hope he has 
forgiven me, and now I am happy!” I never had 
again occasion to look at him with a shade of disap- 
probation. 

I have seen him, sitting by his mother, most seriously 
eonversing on the necessity of supreme love to God, 
and endeavoring to prove that the great God was 
jealous of his honor, and would not approve of any 
idolatry ; when, throwing his arm around his mother, 
and wetting her cheeks with tears, he would say, “My 
dear, dear mother, I am, I fear, your idol, and as you 
are a child of God, I must be separated from you, or 
something dreadful must happen.” 

J could fill a volume with proofs of his piety. He - 
walked in the fear of the Lord all the day long, and he 
loved his Savior tenderly ; his church and his people 
> the objects of his choice: he loved the house of

156 LIFE OF SAMUEL WYKE KILPIN. 


As a Sabbath-school teacher he was regular and 
serious, and felt for the souls of his little class, and 
would address them simply and affectionately. I had 
nearly built a school-room, which was to be opened also 
as a place of occasional worship. Our joys were high 
in the prospect, and the dear boy was to have made his 
first public address on the new year’s day. We had 
formed many plans, and had commenced, planting a 
garden, at the entrance of which he had fixed a weep- 
ing willow, and then came to my study with the his- 
tory of the plantation. Several things were said in 
connection with the young willow and his planting it 
which exceedingly affected our spirits, but I never saw 
him in better health; he was a fine, stout, ruddy youth, 
remarkably strong, and no one could appear more 
likely to live. In afew days after our conversation 
respecting the willow, he complained of weakness and 
great languor, but no apprehension of danger was ex- 
cited. On Wednesday evening I preached a funeral 
sermon, but would not allow him to attend. On return- 
ing, I found my precious boy seriously ill, and carried 
him to bed; he could not walk; so rapidly had disease 
increased, that his senses began to wander. Two phy- 
sicians were called in; both agreed in opinion that no 
danger was near. On the following day the symptoms 
increased, and excited alarm; he had scarcely any in- 
tervals of reason. Once he pointed to the newly-raised 
chapel and cottage, saying, “ In my Father’s house are 
many mansions, to one of which I am going: that 
chapel I shall never enter.” He then sung several 
hymns, and imagined himself in the schools, address- 
ing the children in his usual strains, but was evidently 
unconscious of every thing around him. During that 
night the fever (confirmed typhus) manifested the most 
marked symptoms of approaching death. In the even- 
ing he exchanged earth for heaven, December 4th, 
1818, aged twelve years and eleven months.—My dear 
son’s conversion was as rational as it was scriptural. 


THE END.
